Germany's ,Sweated Women. The astonishing industrial progress made by Germany during the last twentY-five years has been generally attributed to the business capa- city and marVellous business methods of the Germans. The war, however, has opened Brit- ish eyes in more ways than one, and, it has been conclusively proved that the commercial success of 'the• Fatherland is due in great measure to women's sweated labour. Notwith- standing the Kaiser's firm belief that women's sphere of activity is, or should be, limited to the church, kitchen and nursery, he has taken no steps to prevent the exploiting of thousands of his women subjects, struggling for a bare existence in the factories and other industries. The Kaiser has been able to put his millions of soldiers in the field because, thanks to the miserably paid labour of the women, the men could be withdrawn from the occupations of civil life without causing any serious disloca- tion of commerce.

War and Weddings. The war has affected the social life of this country in numerous ways, and many perma- nent changes, some for the better, may result from it. Wedding presents, for instance, had become a social burden, that many people found hard to bear; but the practice has been very considerably curtailed since the outbreak of hostilities, and brides no longer show their presents before, or on the day of the ceremony. Indeed, wedding festivities are now considered out of place, and economy is studied even by the wealthy. The wedding guests are limited to near relations, trains of bridesmaids have dis- appeared, and there are no wedding receptions. Even the time-honoured wedding dress, veil and train are not compulsory, and many fashionable autumn brides have contented themselves with a white dress and hat, and have been attended by only two simbly-dressed bridesmaids.

Long-waisted Effects. Empire waists never go out of fashion alto- gether, for they are graceful and more espe- cially suitable to women of moderate height. Therefore, although the new autumn modes show long-waisted effects, which accord well with wider tunics and skirts, the high waist remains for afternoon frocks, the abnormally , low line being adopted in the. case of tailored suits. Fashion experts predict a revival of Empire styles of all kinds in the near future;

Fish Pie. Flake up about half a pound of cold boiled fish, removing all skin and bone; put it in a pie dish and season with salt and pepper. Pour over the fish two or three tablespoonfuls of milk or fish stock, and half a pint of egg sauce or the remainder of whatever sauce was originally served with the fish. If no left-over sauce is available, some fresh sauce must be made. Rub lib. of boiled potatoes through a sieve, or mash them very thoroughly. Season the mash with pepper and salt, and mix with it a little milk. Cover the pie with this potato crust, smoothing and marking it in ridges with a fork; put some tiny pieces of butter here and there, and bake the pie in a quick oven till lightly browned.

at present they are taking a secondary position. Some afternoon frocks in this mode' have the quaintest of corsages, that just cover the line of the bust; with these a deep tunic that reaches ' almost to the hem of the skirt is worn. This arrangement does not shorten the figure. Eton coats have long basques, generally pleated, in clusters. These are seen on dresses also, and have much to recommend them.

To Renovate Fur Coats. To braid. embroider and to emboss fur seems on a par with painting the lily. Nevertheless, collars, cuffs, and other small pieces of peltry intended for the decoration of evening wraps and coats are thus embellished. Braids of all sorts, as well as soutache, are extensively used on garments of every description. The short shoulder cape of fur is a very smart little acces- sory, when it ends in a point at the back, and just touches the elbows. A model in sable, that had stole ends in front, of brown chiffon velvet, was most attractive and desirable, but its price was in these sad times prohibitive except in the case of the very rich. The flounced fur coat is distinctively handsome, and by means of a hem or flounce, a. last year's coat may be brought quite up to date. A seal musquash coat, for instance, that has had a year or even two year's hard wear, could be converted into a new and fashionable garment if bordered or flounced with skunk, kolinski, or wolf, and the expense would not be serious.

Simple Millinery. A large black velvet hat of quite moderate dimensions and graceful line is steadily gaining ground, although there is at present no reason to suppose that it will supersede the small hat. The two are shown side by side. The larger hat requires very little trimming when the line is good, and then it is decidedly smart. Mounts are happily decreasing in Leight, possibly be- cause exaggeration Of all kinds appear now to be out of place. Velvet, or rather narrow -vel- vet ribbon, has been used for some time as an edging for veils; fur, also very narrow, has taken the place of the velvet on veils intended for winter wear. Either makes a pretty finish and takes from the bareness of the neck when no collar is worn. Soft beaver hats are trimmed with gaily coloured asters; no foliage, however, accompanies the blooms. Metal lace and gold galons are used by milliners, whilst buckles and daggers are again seen on hats.

By MARJORIE.

The Empress Eugenie. The Empress Eugenie has lived to see France, Britain and Russia united in crushing the Power that brought about the downfall of her husband's throne. No one is following the course of the war with keener interest than the venerable Empress, who has turned a wing of her beautiful house at Farnborough into a hos- pital for wounded officers. She bears the entire expense of upkeep, and pays daily visits to the patients, who mostly belong to the Aldershot district which Farnborough Hill overlooks. The Empress's one great hope is to live to see the French Army enter Berlin and thus avenge the humiliation of 1870.

St. John Ambulance Association. Most people are acquainted with the work of the St. John Ambulance Association, but few are familiar with the extent of its activities. More than 5,000 St. John orderlies have been despatched to the front, together with about 20 motor ambulances, of which the Duchess of Somerset and the Duchess of Abercorn have each presented one, as private gifts to the Order. The history of this great organisation, which is one of the oldest in the world, dates from the year 1048. It was founded by a mer- chant named Amalfi, to protect, and, if neces- sary, to help sick pilgrims at Jerusalem. Its patron saint was then a Greek patriarr'), not as at present St. John the Baptist. L pros- pered exceedingly, and' in the middle of the twelfth century occupied magnificent buildings near the Church of the Holy Sepulchre. When Jerusalem was captured, the knights removed to Plotemais, which they re-named St. Jean d'Acre, and a hundred years later they made Cypress their headquarters. In 1310 they were at Rhodes, and as they were enriched by much of the property of the knights templars, they were then very wealthy. Later they settled in Malta. Their red cross on a white gropd has eight points, each of which stands for a beatitude.

HOME NOTES:

/ Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

When frying potato cakes, put a teaspoonful of vinegar into the frying lard. This will pre- vent the cakes from being too greasy when cooked. When a boot or shoe pinches; dip a sponge in hot water, and put it on the part of the shoe pinching. This will expand the leather and make it much more comfortable. A few drops of good perfume dropped on small pieces of pumice stone and put among clothes, ' keeps away moths. Sprinkle clothes for ironing with hot water. It is thus damped more evenly, and is ready sooner for ironing. One teaspoonful of vinegar is a substitute for an egg, and makes a cake light in which drip- ping has been used instead of butter.

Baked Beans. Take two ounces of uncooked beans, prepare as in previous recipe, add to them an equal part of bread crumbs or plain boiled rice, place in a buttered dish, scatter bread crumbs on the top, and bake in oven half an hour.

Fried Fillets of Herring. Clean, wash and wipe four herrings, cut off the fillets from each and remove the skin care- ' fully. Beat up an egg with a dessertspoonful of milk or water. Season the fillets with salt and pepper, and dip each into beaten egg, or else brush over with egg, then cover them with bread-crumbs. Fry the crumbed fillets in hot fat, clarified dripping or lard, to a golden brown. Take up, drain and dish up the fish. Garnish with quarters of lemon and sprigs of fresh parsley; serve hot.

HERE AND THERE.

She Wished So, Too. "I wish I were an ostrich," said Hicks, angrily, as he tried to eat one of his wife's bis- cuits, but couldn't. "I wish you were," re- turned Mrs. Hicks. "I'd get a few feathers for my hat."

Good for the Dictionary. Little Edna: What is "leisure," mamma? Busy Mamma: It's the spare time a woman has in which she can do some other kind of work, my dear.

A Natural Question. "What is your age, please?" asked the lawyer, who was doing a cross-examining stunt. " I have seen 23 summers, sir," answered the lady witness, who seemed to be carrying weight for age. "Au," rejoined the legal light, "and to how many years have you been blind?"

Pickling Beef. Large joints of beef should be put into pickle. These should lie in pickle for three or four weeks or more, and will keep good for three or f cur weeks after that. Choose a whole round of beef (about 201 b. to 241 b.) and a good lump of fat. Sprinkle it with coarse sugar and salt for three or four days, standing it on a dish from which it can drain. Then put it into an earthen pan which will hold four gallons of water, with a cover that will fit close. To two gallons of water add 21b. of coarse sugar, 21b. bay salt, 21b. common salt, and 4,1 b. saltpetre. , Let the Colck Storage. beef lie in pickle three or four weeks. A Fortunate Woman. "What seems to be the matter with Mrs. Brown? She's ailing so." "Yes, poor thing, she got one of those new- fangled diseases." "She has? My, how fortunate she is, to be sure. Here I've had nothing more stylish than lumbago and I'm three years older than she is."

"BIG BEN."

CHILDREN'S CORNER° Conducted by UNCLE NED. ‘3 30~5(2-d.h2,Cl

"Big Ben," the famous clock at the Houses of Parliament, requires a great deal of atten- tion if it is to he kept in proper working order. The winding process keeps two men busy for five hours three times a week.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, As you will notice below, I have te wel- come two new members to the Corner. It gives me great pleasure to put their names in my Big Book. " What a lot of nephews and nieces you must have," once *rote one of the members. Yes, I have a lot; but there is still plenty of room for more, and I would like to have even more for Christmas. The best way of getting them is by every one of my young friends obtaining one new member each. How jolly that would be. During this week a number of little Belgian children have been brought to Warrington from their own country, because the Germans have spoiled their homes. Some of these children have lost relatives they loved very much. Are you sorry for them? Would you feel happy if you were in their place? I am afraid you would he miserable, but we British like to brighten their lot, so that now all must do their share towards making them comfortable. Have you said anything to teacher yet about making a collection of useful things for the Belgians? I would like to know what you are doing in this matter. Every week while the war lasts I will give you an account of at least one brave deed per- formed on the battlefield. It is the stories of these heroes that thrill us al make us proud that -we belong to Britain. Your loving

THE FLIGHT OF SUMMER. (By JESSIE Slum.) Fair Summer! Haste not yet away: Stay, stay. The wintry days will soon be here, Dark, dreamt Fain would we cherish every flower An hour.

They are our fellows, though their form, Forlorn, Lasts but a fleeting hour, all brief, Whilst grief Remains. We, too, shall pass away, Some day.

\ A BRAVE FRENCHMAN.

- —•••- - -- Every day we hear stories of brave deeds which have been perfprmed .on the battlefield. One of the tales which have been published shows how gallant are our French friends. Soon after the war had commenced Lieut. Bruyant, a French soldier, was scouting in dangerous coun- try.- With him were six of his fellow soldiers. SuddenlY 27 Uhlans came on the scene. Although the enemy were stronger in number, they were, not stronger in courage, and the lieutenant had hard work to keep his men from making a dash at the Germans. The Germans, however, did not want to fight, and after a few shots they made tracks for a neighbouring wood. Lieut. Bruyant did not want to lose the oppor- tunity of killing some of the Germans. He realised that if they got to the wood that oppor- tunity would be lost, so lie gave the order to charge. The French soldiers, like dogs loosened from a chain, bounded forward, and were soon on the heels of the cowardly Uhlans, shouting and flashing their swords as they ran along. The courage of the French struck fear into the enemies' hearts, and they attempted to get away, as best they could. However, Lieut. Bruyant and his men reached the thlans and killed the majority of them. The rest rushed away as though 1,000 men were chasing them instead of seven. fi The lieutenant, for his plucky action, was the ,rst, during this war to receive the Cross of the L.egion of Honour.

FARM AND GARDEN.

THE TIME TO PAY. For those who have money to invest the pre- sent time offers many inducements to "go in and win," whether it be in Consols, industrial undertakings, or pedigrte farming stock. With regard to the latter, the situation seems to present a golden opportunity, seeing that the buyers for export, who always try to extract the plums from the pudding, will not be competing, at any rate to an appreciable extent, at the autumn sales. Not only are good animals now selling at reasonable figures, but everything points to the coming of a great boom in all high-class stock when the world is once again at peace. As to horse-breeding nothing need be. said, its future during the next few years is too well assured. Pedigree cattle of all our splendid breeds will be in world-wide demand, while the advantage of useing pure•bred bulls is being well advertised at home by Government action. The scarcity of sheep, both here and in North America, is a pretty safe guarantee that high pyices must prevail, and that skilful breeding will meet with its due reward for years ito come. Many of the sheep sales are over now. ; but the autumn cattle sales shooid attract . farmers more than they have ever done before.— [ " Old Breeder," in "Farm and Home."

FOXES AND POULTRY. The committee of the Masters of Foxhounds Association have passed the following resolu- tion, which has been circulated to all musters and hunt secretaries:—" That cwing to the fact that all foreign supplies of eggs and poultry vill be very much curtailed on account of the war, thereby endangering the national food supply, it is imperative that all masters of hounds should make special endeavours to reduce the number of foxes in the different hunts below the normal."

LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE CROPS. The Board of Agriculture reports that, on the whole, the corn crops of Lancashire and Che- shire have been got in under favourable condi- tions. Except in the hilly districts of North Lanca- shire, all the corn has now been secured. In North Lancashire a good crop of sound potatoes is being lifted, the bulk being now secured. Further south crops are not so good, and in some cases they have ripened prematurely. A yield -about 5 per cent. over average is expected in this county. Some disease exists in every district of Che- shire, but an average crop is still anticipated. Turnips and swedes are healthy and have done well during September, but the crop will hardly reach an average. Mangels also are healthy and vigorous, and the yield will probably slightly exceed the average. ) Crops have not done so well in the eastern as in the western counties.

IN THE GARDEN.

BUSH FRUITS INCREASING. The best time to put in cuttings of bush fruits, such as gooseberries and currants, is as soon as the leaves have fallen. If the gooseberry mildew has been present do not on any account propagate from such trees; rather grub them up aid procure cuttings from a clean stock. In the chse of gooseberries and red currants, well ripened shoots a foot long may be selected, re- moving all the eyes to, say, half the length of the shoot so as to guard against the sucker trouble. This removal of the buds is not neces- sary in the case of black currants, suckers in their case being all important, as by the aid of these the tree can be, so to speak, entirely re- newed. After the cuttings have been cut at a joint they can then be laid in lines in a border of rather sandy soil. If all goes well they should by next autumn be sturdy little plants, which can be transferred to their permanent positions, which should have been deeply dug and well-manured some time in advance, so as to allow of the settling of the soil.

LONG-STALKED FLOWERS. No matter how beautiful a flower may be, its beauty is lost if gathered with a short stalk. The Michael ghi daisy in its many forms, and the useful earTY-fiowering chysanthemums, the varieties of which are now so numerous and varied in colour, are all the more welcome and far more effective when cut with long stems. During the past week the early-flowering chry- santhemums allowed to grow naturally and un- disbudded have been most useful for the filling of vases, &c., in the house, a great point in their favour being the length of time they last in good condition if attention is paid to filling up the vases with water every second day or so.— "Wick," in "Farm and Home."

DEFECTIVE DISTRIBUTION. It is generally recognised that the small hold- ing movement in this country is formidably shackled by the inefficient methods of distribu- tion which prevail. The weakness of the exist- ing system was strikingly demonsttated this summer. On several occasions Covent Garden Market was glutted with fruit and vegetables, and sales were made at ruinous returns. Yet five miles away the consumers were called upon to pay high prices for these commodities. No system of colonising our countryside will be complete without the whole machinery of dis- tribution being thoroughly overhauled, and many of the obsolete parts• sent to the scrap heap. A few years ago a Departmental Com- mittee, appointed by the Board of Agriculture, held an exhaustive inquiry into the existing methods; several useful recommendations were made. But, alas! these, like thany other earnest hopes of Government appointed Committees, repose, dust-stained, in the archives of White- hall.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

BULBS FOR HOSPITALS. Various suggestions have been made for alle- viating distress in the many homes which have come under the war cloud. Sick and wounded soldiers are now returning to these shores to enter the various hospitals. It is admitted that the influence of flowers on a sick body and mind is not inconsiderable. I would venture, therefore, to lay before all flower-lovers whose duty lies at home a sug- gestion that an opportuttity of usefully serving their fellows lies in the cultivation of bulbs in pots, and the resultant distribution of pots of bulbs among the'Various hospitals. They would be welcomed and appreciated by those whose sad lot it has been to pass through the experi- ence of the horrors of a battlefield; while at the same time it would materially help the seeds- man and nurseryman in his praiseworthy en- deavour to continue to keep his assistants in full employment, thus minimising possible dis- tress.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticul- tural Review."

EDUCATION BY ASSOCIATION.

Mr. J. A. Ransome on Adult

School Work.

Mr. J. A. Ransome, president of the Warring- ton Adult School Sub-union, delivered an address on Sunday to the Sankey Bridges (Men's) School on "Education, Evangelism, and Service." 'I lie speaker said these three words were the key-notes of the Adult School Move- ment all over the country. As yet the numbers In Warrington were small compared with other industrial centres. Every Sunday morning in Britain over 100,000 men met to stivly and dis- cus's some aspect of Christianity implied in the subject of the lecturette. Mr. Ransome defined education as a training and drawing out of the national powers and abilities of a man or woman, and not a mere cramming in of knowledge. The object in the Adult Schools was to train the character. Schiller said "Any training of the intellect de- serves attention only so far as it rests on char- acter, because the way to the head is opened through the heart." There is no man so good and high, no man so bad and low, as not to require education at the beginning, all through, and down to the end of his life. There are two movements at present coming into prominence which, in the future, are likely to play a most important part in the national life—the Workers' Educational Association and the Adult Schools. There is a real affinity of aims I and spirit between the two organisations. Both of them are seeking to carry out Mazzini's ideal of "education by association." On broad lines education must first deal with the body. If the body is neglected the other parts of the man do not work well. The body is like a machine, and must be kept in good repair, oiled, clean and adjusted. The most important part of the body is the brain, with its motor, sensory and asso- ciation centres. Any education worthy of the name must tend to the development, control and harnessing of these powers. Then, secondly, there is the mind, which is commonly sub- divided into intellect, emotion and will. The intellect furnishes man with the faculties of 'perception, of imagination, of memory and of conception. The emotions include pleasure, pain, joy, grief, fear, courage, hatred and love, moral sentiment and sympathy, and, governing both intellect and emotion, is the will. Thirdly, man has a soul or spirit, a consciousness, a something which is the citadel of man. Such is the being whom it is desired to educate, and no two beings afe alike. Every individual has to be studied separately, and the best in him nur- tured and trained. This can be done in many ways—by seeking to awaken the activities of the mind, by training the intelligence by suggestive discussions on all subjects which have to do with a man's life, fostering the observation by summer rambles in the country, by lecture schools, by study circles, by fellowship and by a variety of other ways. No cast-iron academic system is desired or required. Your -average working man does qot readily take to book learning, he lacks the' technique and often the desire. But he learns from experience, from persons, from observation, and in time will be- come interested in the "humanities "—biology (the science of life), literature, history, and citizenship. In the Sunday morning schools much intensive work in character building of this kind is being done quietly but surely. Mr. Ransome next spoke on the word Evan- gelism. The strict meaning of the word is "good ne.ws." What is the fundamental and primary element in the Christian evangel? It is that Christ has revealed God, has unfolded in some measure EiS purposes and the ways in which they are fulfilled, to enlarge man's vision of divinity, to show the oneness of the whole human family under a Great Fatherhood. Then Christ revealed what man is, and what he may become through fellowship. In the idea of service the Christian ideal reaches culmination. It is an organic expres- sion of the new life within. The application of this idea reaches every sphere of human activity in seeking to bring about a fuller realisation of the kingdom of God on earth. Ai the close of the lecture a real Adult School discussion took placrA. I CALM HEADS AND STOUT HEARTS. I The first of the monthly " Firesides " to be held during the winter took place at the Long- ford Adult School, under the auspices of the Education Committee of the Sub-Union, on I Sunday afternoon. The speaker was Mr. W. A, I C. Landon, a member of Stockton Heath Adult School, who took for his subject, "The War, from the German point of view." At the outset the speaker said he had found it very difficult indeed to find any unanimity of opinion in Ger- many. He had come to the conclusion, from a .study of the evidence within reach, that at least three shades of opinion existed on the war. First, there was that held by those like the Social Democrats, who were entirely opposed to the militarism of the Junker class, and were ardent believers in peace; secondly, there was that of the militarists, who held with Von `Bernhardi that war was "a biological neces- sity"; thirdly, there was a party who took a middle course, whose opinions had been ex- pressed in the recent speech of the German Chancellor in apologising for the breaking of the treaty with Belgium. The purpose of the conference—including a large attendance of men and women from all the Warrington Schools was served, for, in spite of a position bristling with difficulties, Mr. Landon succeeded in giving a lucid account of German opinion on the war. A very helpful discussion afterwards took place. The Chairman, Mr. John A. Ransome, sounded a warning not to be hustled by the accounts of blood and ruin appearing in the daily Press. During these terrible days calm heads and stout hearts were needed.

A WAR LIBRARY.

A new move- h-f-is.Vee;-;,-(,;a-e--1y the Stockton Heath Men's School in the formation of a War Library. The idea is, to get the members to study for themselves he making of modern Europe, the causes of the .ar, the charalc?r of the belligerents, and the lines on which a just settlement may eventually be possible. Sarolea, Von Bulow, Dawson, Bernhardi, and Ussher are amongst the writers on Germany who will be studied. Another book to be considered is the one written by a number of Oxford scholars, entitled "Why we are at war." This volume contains a reprint of the German White raDer. Maurice Barings' booklon the "Mainsprings of Russia" is an example of the literature which helps to an appreciation of other nationalities. A number of lecturettes will be given at the school on these topics during the next few weeks. Last Sunday morning Mr. George Fleet gave a talk on "The Wonders of Nature." A great deal of interesting information was given by an expert gardener 'on the growth of plants. The subjects ranged from tapioca to linseed, and from cinnamon to sugar-cane. Sensitive plants and exploding gorse, and Nature's methods of seed distributing came under review, so that no one went away without some fresh informa- tion.

ADULT SCHOOL MEN IN CAMP. There are so many Adult School members with the Warrington Territorials that they are able to hold meetings as Successfully as if they were at home. Next week "The Examiner" will give a report of their proceedings.

THE WAR BUDGET.

No. 9 of " The. War Budget," like its prede- cessors, supplies an unequalled contemporary record of the latest incidents in the great struggle, all in pictures. Among the leading features illustrated in this issue are "The Bombardment and Fall of Ant- werp," "The Flight from the Huns in Bel- gium," "The Welcome of the London Scottish in France," "Canada's Reply to the Kaiser," and "How News Travels in War Time." "The War Budget," published every Thurs- day at 3d., by the "Daily Chronicle," consists of 36 pages (including a striking cover in colour), filled with photographs gathered by special correspondents in all parts' of Europe, and wherever in the world a, war is being waged. Such incidents as the camera cannot record are illustrated by a staff of artists from the descrip- tions of eye-witnesses.

A CONTRAST.

The first sea fight on record occurred in 665 a.c.—over 2,500 years ago. In those days they threw stones, at one another. Nowadays it costs about .2300 every time a big naval gun is fired, and the largest gun used by the British Navy can throw a shell weighing nearly a ton as far as l'rom ten to twelve miles.

HINTS ON HEALTH. Useful Information in Popular Language.

Consolation for the Red-nosed. According to "The Medical Press and Circu- lar" of London, a red nose is by no means a sign of drunkenness, and is as common among teetotalers as tipplers. Indigestion is respon- sible almost more than anything elSe for red noses, while excessive tea drinking is apt to 'play havoc with the complexion in general and with the nose in particular. Sometimes the congested nose is a sign of some serious dis- order of the heart, or it may point to a sluggish circulation. The habit of inhaling cigarette smoke and puffing it through the nostrils may contribute to the external wealth of colour.

Swallowing Small Objects. Every mother knows the danger of children swallowing small objects, and is terribly alarmed when she finds that baby has perhaps swallowed a large bead or pebble. The first thought is to give the child a purgative. This is a mistake. If the object swallowed is rough- edged the child should have some new bread given it to eat. Not too much, as this may defeat the, spa!, in view, wiuich is ,to make the object pass naturally and quickly through the intestine. Until the object has been passed the child should be fed on soft food, bread and milk, or porridge, and the motions examined frequently. If possible send for a doctor im- mediately, especially if the child shows any evidence of colic, or seems in great pain, as this indicates that the object has become fixed in some part of the intestines, and a doctor will at once set about removing the cause of the trouble. Good Food and. Pure Air. It is the duty of the housewife to supply her family with two things—good food and pure air. The only way to have plenty of pure air in a house is to make sure of fresh supplies to take the place of that which has been spoiled by use. In the living rooms a bright fire in the grate is a help in keeping up a sweet atmosphere, because a good current of air is passing up the chimney, to replace which air is coming in from the outside. A window opened at the top will facilitate the entrance of the air, and if this causes too much of a draught a screen may he arranged in front of the window which will direct the current of air toward the ceiling. In the bedroom there is no fire burning, hence it is more essential that the window should be open to make sure of proper ventilation. If there is only one window, the best way is to open it both at the top and at the bottom, thus ensuring a proper current. We spend one-third of our lives in our bedrooms. Let us be sure that we do not poison ourselves for eight hours daily by breathing over and over again germ-laden, used- up air.—Dr. Olsen.

'PHONE No. 79.

RECEIVED INTO THE MINISTRY.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. O. P. Pitt Ordained at Wycliffe

Church.

An impressive ceremony took place at Wycliffe Congregational Church on Monday evening, when Mr. C. P. Pitt, the assistant pastor, was ordained to the Congregational ministry. The Rev. T. T. James presided over a large attend- ance, and there were also present the Revs. Principal W. H. Bennett, Professor Mackintosh (of the Lancashire College), John Yonge, A. J. Viner (secretary of the Lancashire County Union), J. Ross Murray and J. G. Gascoigne (of Manchester), Messrs. E. D. Paterson (of the Lancashire College) and C. Eccles (secretary of Wycliffe Church). The Chairman said he was glad to have Mr. Pitt as his colleague. The chief note of interest to him was that Mr. Pitt came from the college which was dear to all their hearts. He believed that the best work in the ministry of Jesus Christ was done by those who, like his colleague, felt that the work was a call which they dared not disobey.

AMPLE SCOPE FOR USEFUL WORK. Mr. C. Eccles made a statement, on behalf of Wycliffe Church, as to the sphere of Mr. Pitt's labours. They started the Cockhedge Mission services in 1870, he said, and the effort was very successful. In the course of time, however, the surroundings became unsuitable, and so a new church was built in Elmwood-avenue, being opened in September, 1913. In that growing neighbourhood there was ample scope for useful work. Twelve months after the opening of the church Mr. Pitt accepted a call to the ministry, and now, as he commenced his labours, they ex- tendel a- sincere welcome to him. The Re`v. J. Ross Murray said that it had been his privilege to be Mr. Pitt's ministTr before lie entered Lancashire College. The esteem in which Mr. Pitt was•helti by his former fellow- members was evidenced by the fact that many of them had come over from Manchester, in order to. be present at that ceremony. In his home-life, Mr. Pitt was a dutiful son and an affectionate brother, whilst in church-life he was a most reliable and trustworthy member. It had been a great pleasure to him (the speaker) to see Mr. Pitt enter the ministry, and be felt that in coming to Warrington he would enjoy the faithful comradeship of his friend, Mr. James.

THE MESSAGE OF FELLOWSHIP. The Rev. A. J. Viner said he came with the message of fellowship. He represented what they were sometimes apt to fore—the wider relationship of their churches. He would like to remind the church and Mr. Pitt that there was a great inspiration in the wider horizon. Though they had their individual parts, there was a cleasure and a delight in fellowship. At the same time, a faithful local pastorate was the best way of serving the general community, and, passing into the ministry of the Church, , Mr. Pitt would realise the joy of service. He counselled him to try to make each unit as efficient as possible, so that the weak would feel strong, and the strong even stronger. Mr. E. D. Paterson said that he not only - came as a representative of Lancashire College, but also as a personal friend of Mr. Pitt's. Therefore he could tell them something of that gentleman's real worth. He was one of the truest and most reliable members of the college. Whatever Mr. Pitt said, they might be sure he said from the bottom of his heart, for they had in him a strong, a true and a loving man. The Rev. Professor Mackintosh gave an ex- position of Congregational principles, empha- sising the fact that the Church had an ideal, and sought to preserve in the best way possible the principles of the New Testament Church. Mr. C. P. Pitt then made a formal statement of his belief, prefacing it with an wpression of thanks to all those friends who had, in the years gone by, encouraged and , helped him in hi 9 work. , The Rev. J. G. Gascoigne offered the ordina- tion prayer, after which the Rev. Principal W. H. Bennett gave the charge to the new minister..

ODDFELLOW HONOURED.

Past Grand's Certificate for Bro.

H. Bentley.

On Saturday a pleasant evening was spent by the members of ,the "Olive Branch" Lodge of the Warrington District of the Manchester Unity of Oddfellows, the occasion being the presentation to Past Grand Bro. Henry Bentley of a Past Officer's Certificate, suitably framed. An additional interest was lent to the proceed- ings' by the fact that they synchronised with the official visit of the District Officers. The meeting was presided over by the Noble Grand of the Lodge (Bro. John J. Cook), and amongst those present were: District Grand Master Bro. Henry Lawrence, Deputy G.M. Bro. Edward ,Spann, I.P.P.G.M. Bro. Geo. Higham, P.P.G.A.I. Bro. Frank Webster (District Secre- tary), L.G.M. Bro. Wm. Hammon, P.G. Bro. Harry Broadhurst, sen., P.G. Bro. John Brown, p.p.G.M. Bro. Peter Lester, P.P.G.M. Bro. Alban Boyle (secretary of the lodge), and Bro. Arthur Cotterill, P.G. (treasurer), and other past and present officers connected with the various lodges in the district. The District Grand Master, in making the pres- entation, said he was glad to see so many young members present. that night. It clearly showed that they were taking an interest in the work of the society, but they must make their influ- ence felt in bringing young members to the lodge.

Young Members Wanted as Office Bearers. They wanted young members to take up offices in the lodge; in fact, in all the lodges in the district. It gave him (the GM.) the utmost pleasure to ask P.G. Bentley to accept the certi- ficate, which was intended as a slight recogni- tion of his valuable services to his lodge. He hoped the recipient would continue to help for- ward the good work the lodge was doing, and that the certificate would always remind him of the pleasant and useful evenings he had spent in the " Olive Branch" Lodge. (Applause.)

P.G. Bentley, in acknowledgment, said that he was proud to receive the Past Officer's Certi- ficate. He had always had a desire to obtain one, and had made up his mind to work hard for his lodge and was pleased to find that his efforts had been considered by the officers and members of his lodge as worthy of an emblem. He thanked them very sincerely for their kind- ness. (Applause.) P.G. Bro. John Brown, after extending a hearty welcome to the District Officers on behalf of the lodge, spoke words of praise with regard to P.G. Bentley's Services to the lodge, and hoped his example would be emulated by other young members. (Applause.)

LODBE MEMBERS AT THE FRONT. Deputy G.M. Bro. Spann, after compliment- ing Bro. Bentley on his work in connection with Oddfellowship, said the "Olive Branch" Lodge might well be proud of the officers who carried on ...the work of the lodge, and also of the fact that a fair number of its members were now doing their duty in various capacities in defence of their country. (Applause.) The C.S. of the District (P.P.G.PiI. Bro. Web- ster) gave particulars regarding the progress of the society in general, Which, he said, was very satisfactory indeed. He predicted a prosperous future-for the Manchester Unity of Oddfellows.

• (Applause.) A hearty vote of thanks was accoriled to the District Officers, on the proposition of P.G. Bro.

• Seed. During the eveningtsongs were contributed .hy the following: Bros. P.G. Walter Bennett, P.G. Thomas Seed, and P.P.G.M. Alban Boyle, P.G. Bro. Arthur Cotterill presiding at the piano.

ST. • PAUL'S. Good That Will Issue Out of the War.

Harvest festival services were held at St. Paul's. Church, Warrington, on Sunday. There were large congregations throughout the day,' and more particularly in the evening, when every seat was occupied. The sacred building had been nicely decorated for the occasion. Preaching in the evening from the text "Lift up your eyes and look unto the fields, for they are already white unto the harvest" (St. John iv. 25) the Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) said that some people went to harvest festival ser- vices simply to look at the decorations, whilst others Went merely because it was the custom. To such his message was: "If you want the Blessing of God you must lift Up your eyes,' you must look above, from whence, alone, comes strength; you must look to Him Who is the Giver of all things—the God of Life and the God of Mercy. One thought that he would specially like to urge upon his hearers was the thought that the time of harvest was a testing time. Just as they had gathered in the wheat from the seed which they had sown, so would they gather in from the spiritual seed which they sowed. The young man who said that he was sowing his wild oats and would reap wheat by and by was predicating something that was contrary to the laws of Nature and all human experience; he was trying to reap something exactly different to what he sowed, and that never could be done. Continuing, the preacher said that when they looked on the terrible war that was now raging on the Continent they surely, believed from the bottom of their hearts that good would come out of it. And what was that good— what was the harvest they were going to reap out of all that turmoil and pain? One thing lie was perfectly sure they would reap, and that was the Brotherhood /of Man. All the big social troubles in the past had been a great I curse to the nation, but he believed the war I was—and would be—the means of bringing all classes closer and closer together, and that one outcome of the Titanic struggle would be that the nations would learn something of the true Brotherhood of Man. Another great lesson that the war was teaching was that carelessness and indifference to the cause of God did not pay. If they were naughty children, then God, as a faithful and true parent, must punish them for their naughtiness. At a time like this they must not be grateful simply by word of mouth; their gratitude must go a little deeper than that—they must express it by giving of their substance. The clergyman's life was not a picnic, and the clergyman could not live on nothing, and therefore he appealed to his hear- ers to be liberal in their gifts to the cause of God in that parish, remembering that true religion was not only in the head and heart, but also in the pocket. During the service the choir sang the anthem "0 clap your hands together." The preacher in the morning was the Rev. IL P. S. Collier, Vicar of Padgate. At a children's service in the afternoon the Vicar preached, whilst the Rev. 11. Downham gave an address to the members of the United Bible Classes.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
STHETTON.

Harvest thanksgiving services were held at the Stretton Parish Church on Sunday, when the offertories were devoted to the Belgian Relief Fund and the Choir Fund. The Rev. W. H. Palin occupied the pulpit in the morning and the Rev. H. G. Thompson in the afternoon, whilst the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross (vicar) was the preacher in the evening. The church was tastefully decorated and the choir,rendered special music.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
WESLEYAN CHURCH, WINWICK.

Harvest thanksgiving services were held in the above place of worship on Sunday, con- ducted in the afternoon aml evening by the Rev. A. J. Shields, Didsbury College. Miss Whitmore, of Warrington, rendered solos at both services, which were highly appreciate. The attendance was very much above the aver- age, a greater interest having been shown in the place all round since being taken over by the Wesleyan Connexion. On Monday evening the usual social entertainment, with disposal of fruit, flowers, and vegetables, was held. Mr. Shallcross, sen., presided: A very interesting programme of songs, recitations, &c,, was pro- vided by Messrs. Sephton, Earlestown; Airs. Lawson, the Misses Louie and Madge Ormiston, and Mr. Halliwell. Miss Ormiston presided at the organ at all the services.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
lIILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHURCH.

•••••

Harvest thanksgiving servites were held on Sunday, when there were good congregations. The ladies of the congregation had beautifully decorated the church. The preacher, morning and evening, was the Rev. George Curtis. Mrs. James Kenworthy was the choir leader, and Mr. Harrison the organist. The anthems given by the choir were "0 clap your hands together" and "While the earth remaineth," Miss Capell taking the solo. A duet was given by Mrs. Kenworthy and Miss Capell, "0 lovely peace," and Miss Taylor gave a beautiful solo, "Thanks- giving," all the special singing being ,frhighly appreciated. A Young People's Service was held in the afternoon, presided over by Mr. J. Taylor. A* appropriate address was given by Mr. T. Deakin. The children delighted those present with their sweet singing, Miss Challoner presiding at the instrument. Two beautiful solos were ably rendered by Miss Bates. . _ .

On Monday evening a social was held in the Schoolroom, presided over by the Rev. George Curtis. The musical items were :—Pianoforte duet, "Poet and Peasant," the Misses Chal- loner ; solo, "My little grey home in the West," Miss Capell ; glee, "Oh, who will o'er the Downs so free?" choir; Master A. Mayhall delighted with his violin solo, and a duet, "Friendship," by Mrs. Kenworthy and Miss Capell, was greatly applauded. During the evening Mr. J. Moss acted as am-

tioneer. The social was brought to a close by the singing of "God save the King." The offerings for the Sunday amounted to .85 Bs. 7d.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. CLEMENT'S MISSION.

The harvest thanksgiving services at St. Clement's Mission were-concluded on Sunday, being conducted by the Rev. T. Vickers, curate- in-charge, and the Rev. A. H. Johnson, of Wigan Parish Church. The attendances were good, and the collections, which were taken in aid of the Assistant Clergy Fund, realised £5. In the evening special music was rendered by the choir, including the anthem, "Let the earth bring forth fruit," the solo parts being taken by Messrs. Berry and Beswick.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
FELL INTO MOVING MACHINERY. Peaketh Thresher's Terrible Fate.

The sad story of a man's fall into the drum of a threshing machine, and of the fatal conse- quences following thereon, was related at an inquiry, held at the Coroner's Court on Monday afternoon, by Mr. S. Brighouse, into the circum- stances attending the death of Hugh Pownall, of Brookside Cottages, Penketh.

Mr. T. S. Steel Was present to watch the in- terests of the widow, and Mr. Allen, of Wigan, followed the case on behalf of Mr. Walter Fitter, of Hood-lane, Sankey, the owner of the machine.

Fred Pownall, a gardener, living in Station- road, Penketh, said the deceased, who was his brother, was 34 years of age. About seven o'clock on the previous Thursday night witness was informed that his brother had met with an accident. Witness visited the Warrington In- firmary to see his brother, and Dr. Law told him that it was a very serious case, and an operation would be necessary. Witness saw his brother, who was conscious, and who, referring to the accident, said, "I was sweeping up and finishing for the night, when I got caught." The Coroner: Did he say anything Further?— Witness: No. Witness, continuing, said he again visited the Infirmary at 9.30 the following morning, when his brother appeared to be sinking, and at 12.30 witness was notified that death had taken place.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
JUST FINISHING FOR THE ISAY.

William Reid, of Newfield Cottages, Lymm, an engine driver employed by Mr. Walter Fit- ter, said that the deceased and himself were both working at Cuerdley Hall Farm, Penketh. on October Bth. At 6 o'clock witness and Pownall were both standing on the top of the machine. It was getting dusk and they were just finishing work for the day. The engine was still running: and witness told Pown'all to sweep the rubbish into the feeding drum. He made to do so, but as he was getting the brush he fell into the drum. The engine was stopped and after first aid had been rendered, deceased was removed to the Infirmary..

_ Questioned by Mr. Allen, witness said the machine was a self-feeder. _

Eva Baxter, a nurse at the Infirmary, stated that death took place at 11 o'clock on the morn- ing of October 9th. _

A verdict of "Accidental death" was re- turned, and the jury expressed their sympathy with the widow.

Mr. Allen also expressed sympathy, on behalf of his client, and said that the machinery was of the most up to date character. The accident was one of those things that could not be guarded against. Mr. Steel acknowledged the expressions of sympathy on behalf of the widow. There were, he said, three children, the youngest of whom was two months old. It was to be hoped that something substantial would be done for the family. On Tuesday the remains of the late Mr. Pownall were interred in the Great Sankey Cemetery. Wreaths were sent from numerous sympathisers, including one from the employes of the Penketh Tanning Company.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SPARE GAMES

For Soldiers In Homes And Hospitals.

To provide amusement and recreation for wounded soldiers during their convalescent stage in homes and hospitals, a games bureau has been organised by the editor of "The Tatler " to supply indoor games of all kinds.

There must be— a vast number of houses in Great Britain which have cupboards contain- ing games such as dominoes, draughts, jig-saw- puzzles, patience, race games, chess, &c., ,which are not now in use. These, if sent in, will be distributed for the use of the convalescent sol- diers and sailors.

Mrs. Hugh Spottiswoode, who has consented to act as honorary superintendent of the bureau, is getting into touch with all the hos- pitals and convalescent homes at home and abroad, and will be grateful if any "Examiner" readers can send a contribution to her at the " Tatler " Games Bureau, Great New-street, London, E.C. All the parcels will be sent free of expense of distribution to the various institutions, and the donors' names will be acknowledged in the pages of "The Tatler." All packages sent should have the name and address of the donors plainly written on the outside.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
METEOR LIBELLED.

Mistaken for a German Airship.

WARRINGTONIANS' THEORIES.

One of the blackest nights I have ever known was last Tuesday's—the night of the mysterious noise.

I was cycling at a comfortable speed along the Knutsford-road at High Legh, and was musing upon the recklessness of a motorist who had just passed—or, rather, just missed—me, when a reddish glare flashed across the heavens. "That's a funny colour, mate," came a voice from the darkness, but as the tramp spoke—for I believe it was a tramp—a second flash occurred, this time a little brighter. I replied in the affirmative, and as I' rode on, thinking of a new-fashioned lightning, a noise as of a distant explosion came through the air. It sounded like the passing of a fast train over a high bridge—but miles away. The pheasants near the wood on Colonel Legh's estate uttered a shriek, and from the distance came the .barking of a few dogs. Highly strung and afraid—not of Germans, but that a big explosion had occurred in Warrington—l rode on faster and faster. From which way the noise came I could not tell. Perhaps something had happened at the Gas Works. Perhaps it was at Northwich and would not affect War- rington. Perhaps it was at the works at Irlam. Whatever it was, I believed something terrible had happened "somewhere." My first thought of a disaster at the War- rington Gas Works seemed corroborated, for, from an elevated portion of the road, no sem- blance of a light could be seen over the town. Ahead of me I could only just discern the two electric lights on the'high standards. at Latch- ford Locks, and they appeared to stand out against the blackness like tiny stars. Probably, to one unacquainted with the road, those lights could have been assigned to an airship. But I knew the road well, and all the King's horses and all his men could not have dragged me into believing that the lights were anything more than those which cast their rays owr the waters near the Locks. Emerging from the darkness, I found that the gas facing the "Dog and Dart" was burning all right. Nothing seemed amiss there, neither did there as I passed through Latchford With- out—only that I met very few Pedestrians. Per- haps they were all at the scene of the explo- sion! But, no! At the car terminus half-a- dozen men were eagerly engaged in a dis- cussion, and, in passing, I heard something about a "bomb and an airship !" My curiosity was aroused, and, disnlounting, I turned back, ;and inquired what was the matter. They related the story of the flash in the sky, and of the explosion, which, to use one of my unknown friend's descriptions, "fairly shook the earth !" It was a story I knew well, but of which I said little. One of the group stated that he had seen a vertical light, which, he was Positive, dangled from some aircraft; "but," he added, "it might have been a British one !" . All reports as to the time of the explosion I coincided. . .

Other people in town were talking about the 1 " unearthly " noise, and of the flash, but it was I very evident that imagination, of the kind possessed by Yankee—and, now, also, German— journalists, had been at work—and in some cases had put in plenty of overtime. More than one Warringtonian had actually seen the airship, and could tell where it passed over, and the direction in which it was travelling. Indeed, I have since been informed of one worthy dame who heard "something like the noise of propellers"! However, my own theory, like many other people's, was to be thrown on the scrap-heap. On the following morning I scanned the papers, and found that the "airship" had been seen, all at the same moment, over such widely scattered areas as Chester, Knutsford, Liverpool and Manchester. Strange! Then the scientists came to the rescue, stating that the description of the mysterious light and the noise of the 'explosion tallied with a meteoric visitation which might occur at this time of the year. After all, somebody was right in saying it was an "unearthly" noise. In the car the passengers forgot about the German investment of Antwerp, for the chief .topic of conversation 114 reference to the previous night's happenings.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NATION'S POSTCARDS.

The war lends itself completely to the produc- tion of picture postcards, and Messrs. Raphael Tuck and Sons, Ltd., have seized upon the op- ix)rtunity promptly and effectively. Under official guidance and sanction this firm have 'published sixty different sets dealing with the Navy. the Army, aviation, &c. The cards tell the stories of our resources with the best pos- sible taste, and in a manner that should lead to a large demand.

PENSIONS FOR BEREAVED.

Trades Council Join in Labour Party's Petition.

On the initiative of Mr. G. N. Barnes, M.P., the Independent Labour party have addressed to the organised workers of the country an appeal for support in pressing upon the Govern- ment the need for making adequate provision for the women and children who through the war may lose their bread-winner or main sup- port. The proposals put forward are that the widow of every soldier killed shall be paid £1 per week; that the mother of every soldier killed, provided she be solely dependent tipon him, shall receive a like amount; that the wife of every soldier engaged in fighting shall get the same sum whilst he is on active service, and that every soldier permanently maimed as the result of his share in the fighting shall be pen- sioned off with twenty shillings per week. The appeal came before the Warrington Prates and Labour Council at their meeting on Tuesday evening, when Mr. Hamblett moved that that body should record their endorsement of the same and join in urging it upon the Government.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
Whilst Our Hearts Are Warm.

At a time like this, he said, the heart of every one of them was warmed up, and they almost felt that to ask for ..t1 a week was a ridiculously inadequate request to make. Surely the Government would not need any pressure to make some provision for those left behind by the men who shed their blood in the country's cause? _ s

Mr. Greely, in seconding, remarked that the matter was of such importance that he thought it ought to be made a test question at the next election.

The Secretary (Mr. Dukes) said he did not think they ought to be compelled to ask for such reasonable provision for those who suffered through the war; they ought to demand it as a right. He could not help but remind the delegates that in this couutry a comparatively few people took a thousand million pounds a year and did no work in return for them; yet it had taken over two months to raise three millions for the relief of those people who had been rendered destitute through the war. The £1 a week proposed by the " Citizen" was going to take some fighting for, and they would only get it just to the extent that they threatened and imperilled the position of the present office holders.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
A BURNING QUESTION.

Mr. Helsby described the question as a burn- ing one, and said there were times when he almost felt that they should do what they could to stop recruiting until the thing was settled. They ought to have said at the first : "If you ask trade unionists to enlist, what are you going to give them?" Mr. Shimmings said he would like to utter a very strong protest against the way soldiers' wives had to go to the Post Office and hang about for their money. Some better arrange- ment should, in his opinion, be devised; the money warrants should be sent to them by post, the same as were the warrants for ex-soldiers' pensions. - The Secretary wondered whether any of the municipal premises—for instance, Parr Hall— could be placed at the disposal of the women, so that they might have somewhere to sit down whilst waiting. The Council decided to give their support to the £1 per week programme.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
ERUPTION ON CHILD'S LIMBS.

74, Cranbrook-road, St. John's, London, S.E., Eng.--" My little girl then five years old had her side badly hurt, the skin being rubbed off. It soon formed a great mattery sore. Her legs at last were great sores all over. To make mat- ters worse my little boy aged eight broke out. His face was the worst. It was a dreadful sight. Then my little girl two and one half years had her face the same in no time. The children were tormented with the terrible irritation. My little boy used to pick at I*l ears so much in his sleep that we 'rally thought he would pick it all away. This trouble would form in mattery pimples and in no time they would break and turn into great ugly scabs. They did not know what it was to get a night's rest for weeks. " I saw the Cuticura Soap and Ointment ad- vertised in the paper so I thought I would try them. The first night they slept better. I bathed them regularly three times a day and dressed the places with the Cuticura Ointment and they were cured." (Signed) Mrs. Mary Lippross, Jan. 22, 1914. Although Cuticura Soap and Ointment are sold by chemists throughout the world, a sample of each with I 32-p. Skin Book will be sent post free upon re- quest. Address post card: F. Newbery and Sons, 27, Charterhouse-square, London, E.C.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
CHURCHES AND THE WAR.

A NATION BENT ON AGGRESSION. Rev. H. Ogden and General Bernhardi's

Book.

At Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday evening, the Rev. H. Ogden preached upon "General Bernhardi and his Book, 'Germany and the Next War,'" taking for his text the whole of the eleventh chapter of the' Book of Daniel. The preacher introduced his subject by saying that no man cared to shed a, cherished delusion. They 'could always find a reason for their prejudices. Whenever a man wanted to believe any pa7ticular thing, he could find sup- port for his belief. He (the preacher) had ever pictured the German proletariat as peaceful and peace-loving citizens. In his imagination he had conceived the German as loitering amongst his old canals and historic buildings; a man to whom music was part of his soul, and to whom rag-time was as a craze. A closer study of the German at home revealed a mili- tarist of a determined order, and, as a student of Bernhardi and Nietzsche, he could be noth- ing less than an aggressor.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
Kaiser a Pet Aversion.

He (the preacher) had ever reckoned the Kaiser as amongst his pet aversions. He was ari Emperor of ability, with a pronounced mania for German aggression. And yet, with all his aggressive movements, this war would have been impossible without a united Ger- many.

A -Congregational professor who had just ar- rived from Germany, asked permission at the close of a service to say a few words to the congregation, and emphasised what others had stated—that behind the Kaiser and his advisers was a united Germany. Britons were evidently under a delusion if they were presuming that there would be a revolution in Berlin when all the truth was known. The Kaiser certainly could have used his influence to prevent war: he might have added another twenty-five years, _ _ .

if he had been spared to live, to his) peace record. Had he used that influence, he would have gained the approbation of Europe.

A People Educated to War._

But he had a people around him educated to war and bent upon aggression. If peace had ensued at this time, foreign chancellories would have been his debtors, but most probably he would have raised a storm at Ihome. The text- book of the German soldieik—and Bernhardi must be regarded as such—postulated that war was the only evidence of a growing and thriv- ing nation. Quoting from Treitschke, Bern- hardi said that it was only the weary, spiritless and exhausted ages which had played with the dream of perpetual peace. It was self-evident, according to Bernhardi, that any nation which sought peace and strove to establish peace amongst the nations of the earth was inviting self-destruction. He even sought to discredit the Hague Peace Congress. It was permissible, in his idea, to submit trivial matters to the Hague Congress, but to abolish war entirely was more than any congress had a right to consider.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
Applying Darwin's Theory.

He introduced Darwin's survival of the fit- test in relation to nations, and considered that war was a biological necessity of the first im- portance. Continuing along that line of thought, he confessed that wars were terrible— but necessary, for they saved the State from social putrefaction and stagnation. He quoted Schiller, who said:

Man is stunted by peaceful days, In idle repose his courage decays. Law is the weakling's game, Law makes the world the same. But in war man's strength is seen, War ennobles all that is mean; Even the coward belies his name.

Still urging the insanity of peace, Bernhardi said to the Germans: "Our people must learn to see that the maintenance of peace never can, or may be, the goal of a policy." He jeered at the great American nation, and ventured to tell them that, because their Government had cham- pioned the ideas of universal peace, the moral development of their national character was im- possible. With Papal assurance he told the United States that, if they advanced further on that road, they would one day pay dearly for such a policy. If further evidence were needed of the war- like character of the German people, it was supplied to them by Mr. A. G. Gardiner, of "Daily News" fame, who told them, in one of his articles, that the German soldier went into battle with a copy of Nietzsche in his pocket.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
The Nietzscheian Doctrine.

"A.G.G." epitomised Nietzsche in a sentence when he said that that philosopher's view was that the universe was "for the aristocrat, for the strong man, fot the bird of prey."

All Brithin and the civilised world, said the preacher, has been startled by the barbarity and systematic cruelty of the Germans. We were amazed at the callous disregard of Belgian neutrality, and all that it has involved. Even a cursory study of the German prophets and teachers makes their attitude plain. Bern- hardi, for example, declares that "right is re- spected so far only as is compatible with advan- tage." It becomes intelligible to us now why treaties are only scraps of paper. On such a basis, they are merely manuscripts, with no principle behind them. In summing up his various views, the writer of "Germany and the Next War" says that expediency "in the higher sense" must alone be conclusive in deciding whether to undertake a war in itself morally justifiable. Right, evidently then, is only to be measured by advantage; and expediency is to settle the time and the occasion of a war.

Teaching that is Morally Foul. Could the teachings of a text-book—and that the text-book of a nation—be more morally foul? Germany has measured us by the same morals as her own. She declares that "perfi- dious Albion" would have violated the neutral- ity of any State if it had ben to her advantage; Germany, corrupt herself, believes all others to be moved by corrupt motives. And all these views are the teachings. of scientific men, de- livered to Germany in the sacred name of culture! _

_ Bernhardi, the rev, gentleman continued, rele- gated Christianity to a‘very subordinate place— indeed; for international relations, it became useless. The claim cif Christian men, that it should be a world relqlon, would be absolutely destroyed by German culture. He said that Christian morality was a good thing, in so far as it affected relationships between men of the same nation, but to attempt to love a man of another race was treason. International Brotherhood—Treason: %.

He boldly said that the love a man showed I to another country as such would imply want of love for his own countrymen. In plain words, a German, by Chlristian instruction, must love Germans, but he was a traitor if he loved a Britisher and Britain. If such a law was to stand good the British must recall all their missionaries, and cease to preach the Gospel to every creature. In conclusion, the preacher said that the reason this country was at war was self-evident. The marvel, after all, was not that the war had come, but that it had been so long delayed. If the type of teaching outlined had soaked into the German mind—and even that was vouched for—they could not wonder at scientific bar- barism. The most disquieting fact of all this reading was that it revealed not only the mind of the Kaiser and his immediate advisers, but the mind and intentions of a united Germany. A young British soldier in Boulogne was asked what he was doing there. His reply was: "Fighting for civilisation !" Never in the his- tory of the world, had Britain been engaged in a holier war. It meant either victory or bar- barism. No young man ought to refuse to take his share in this the hour of his nation's peril. When the morrow of peace came the settlement must be on such terms that never again should any " mailed fist" be allowed to threaten the peace and the harmony of the world. A CALL TO PRAYER.

During evensong at St. Paul's Church, War- rington, on Sunday, the Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) referred to the services of interces- sion held nightly in the church and announced a falling off in the attendances. "Surely," he pleaded, "we are not going to wait for a reverse before we come to pray to God !" The services are held nightly at eight o'clock and every Wednesday and Friday the names of all men at the front known to be connected with the L parish are read out.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
VICAR WHO OFFERED HIMSELF. Bishop Thinks He Should Stay at Home.

Prior to delivering his sermon at St. Paul's harvest festival on Sunday evening, the Vicar (the Rev. W. Bracecamp), chaplain to the local Territorials, made the following personal state- ment to a crowded congregation: "I feel that there is a statement I ought to make to you concerning something which is pressing very heavily upon me at the present moment. It concerns a conflict of opinion within my own mind. Most of you know what a great interest I have taken in connection with the Terri- torials. Most of you also know that from the beginning of the war I offered myself to go any- where at any time; further you know that I got a reply that I was to wait. I have been waiting Cs er since. During this last week I have still been on the point of being appointed as a chap- lain, but, unfortunately, my Diocesan, the I Bishop, does not agree. He says I ought not to leave my parish, and, as he is my superior officer, you can quite understand the feeling that is in my mind at the present moment. I don't think I ought to make a statement to you to-night of exactly what I am going to do; in fact, I don't feel that I can, though pressure has been brought to bear upon me by my best friends, who, I know consider my welfare, ancl think, from the bottom of their hearts, of my interests. I do not, honestly, feel quite in a position to make a statement to you to-night as to what I am going to do. But I venture to think that I can ask this of you—knowing that I have worked among you for twelve years; knowing, too, that we know something of each other, and that, I hope, we love each other— that you will extend to me your prayers at this very serious time.

'1

===== 0002601_19141017_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
The Bishop's Conditions.

The conditions laid down by the Bishop are such that I cannot possibly consent, and then, on the other hand, there is the call of King and country; therefore, you will understand my feelings. I would, if I could, say to you to- night: I will stay behind and do all I possibly can for you, but I can't say it at this moment. Nor can I say to my Bishop that I will stay at this present moment. However, I promise you that at the earliest opportunity I will declare from this pulpit what my feelings are and what my decision is, but I feel that I cannot do it to-night. I ask you from the bottom of my heart to remember me at the Throne of Grace in this trying time. Knowing, as you do, how I have stood up in public and pleaded to the young men of Warrington to sacrifice them- selves for their country, you can understand my feelings now that great pressure has been brought to bear on me, and I am told that I must not go, and that my duty is at home. I can't say any more—l can't trust myself to— but please remember me at the Throne of Grace. It is a joy to me to be able to tell you that my only boy will be going into training in a day or two. Therefore, if I cannot go, I shall have a part of my family there, and I ask that you will remember my boy in your prayers."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
BERNHARDrS IMMORAL TEACHING The Failure of German Social Democracy.

A well-attended meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. Society, held on Runday, was presided over by Mr. Wm. Clegg. The lesson was read by Mr. Corlett, and Messrs. J. Minshull and Wm. Brown led the prayers. During the ser- vice, Madame Fairhurst rendered the solos, "My ships" and "For the children's sake," whilst Mr. J. 'Parton gave "The farewell in the desert." The accompanists were Mrs. Massey and Mr. Ernest Hannan. The band's selec- tions were " Cavatina " and "Cavalleria Rusti- cana."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN PEOPLE'S RESPONSIBILITY.

The Rev. H. Ogden spoke upon " Bernhardi's immoral teaching," and said that, as they faced the facts, they were forced to the conclu- sion that in this war they were fighting not only against the Kaiser and his advisers, but also against a whole country which sanctioned aggression. Militarism had eaten itself very deeply into the national life of Germany. Mr. W. H. Dawson, in his "Germany at Home," said that despite the dislike, and even hatred, of the German officers by the civil population, yet the civilians loved the army. Most of the men had been in barracks, and knew barrack life; the military spirit was strong within them. One was puzzled by the attitude of the democra- tic party in Germany. Mr. W. Mullins, secre- tary of the Cardroom and Blowers' Operative lAssociation, was equally astonished, and de- clared that the German Social Democrats had been false to their own teaching. Mighty as they were in numbers, they had failed to influence the German Government for peace. In conversa- tion with a student who had resided for four years in Germany, he (the speaker) asked why the German democrat had been so impotent in this crisis, and was told that one had but to say "Fatherland !" to the German democrat, and all his democracy was lost in the German. One could only conclude that German democrats were soaked in the doctrines of Bernhardi and Nietzsche. The writer of "Germany and the Next War" opened his book with vain boast- ing about German prosperity. Everyone ad- mitted the marvels which had been wrought in German commerce since 1870. But Germany would certainly have been wise had she been content with her unique place in world com- merce and European politics. Mr. Churchill said that Berlin, for a decade or two, had held the key of peace, and he was surprised that she should risk her unique position in a war.

Is War a Commercial Necessity? But, as all her teachers insisted, there could only be German commerce whilst there was aggressiOn, and commerce and aggression must . go hand in hand. Not only did Bernhardi in- sist that war was necessary for national life, and that right was only commensurate with advantage, but also that war was an indispens- able factor of culture—a culture in which a true civilised natidn found the highest expres- sion of strength and vitality. It was a rather remarkable commentary upon such a doctrine that Goethe, Handel and Mozart were all pro- ducts of the simple German life of the period prior to 1870. So far as English life was con- cerned, it had been taken as axiomatic that cul- ture and peace went hand in hand. Brutality or Bernhardism. Bernhardi had no mercy for smaller nations. He only regarded them as small and effete. He spoke of thdr existence as pitiable, and looked, upon the great power that had any regard for them as "the victim of a curse." Such advan- tages as Belgium and Holland would afford to the Germans were viewed with longing eyes, and it was incomprehensible to the writers of a certain German school that small States should be allowed to bar the way to Imperial progress. Bernhardi said of the smaller races that, without war, they would easily choke the growth of healthy, budding elements, and uni- versal decadence would follow. War, to exter- minate small States, he continued, was as necessary as the struggle of the elements in Nhaadtuf the force Nature. eeit We harteewonder, then, that Belgium of the German invader? But Social Democracy would receive scant pity from Be.rnhardi and his school. He linked them, in his contempt, with the Peace party, and believed that no workman in any country should receive an advance in wages until a similar advance had been granted in neighbouring countries. No British artisan, by that law, would receive a PennY per hour advance until his German confrere had received the advance too. One was all the more amazed that Social Democrat Would give the slightest hearing to such a teacher.. The man who said that might was the supreme right would ever be a menace to the toilers of the earth. Surely the day was coming, and was not far distant, when all such teaching and all such doctrine would be relegated to the oblivion it deserved.

Descriptive. Enthusiast Waiter (explaining the situation): "Let this 'ere meat axe be the Russians a-comin' in on the East; the carvin' knife's the Frenchies along 'ere; our boys in the ;mustard pot; and 'eres the Germane—this 'ere plate o' tripe."—" Punch."

DRINK IN WAR. TIME.

Perhaps it was scarcely to be ex- pected that Warrington woulid take steps to curtail the sale of drink during war time. But Warrington is not the only Place in England that goes placidly along with this enemy within the gates while we have another knocking loudly at the gates. As soon as war broke out the Russians prohibited the sale of vodka, the Ger- mans forbade the sale of liquor to any of its soldiers, and France prohibited the manufacture and sale of absinthe; but we went about our business "as usual." London has closed its public houses first at eleven o'clock, and now at ten o'clock, instead of after mid- night, and other towns have made.in- roads upon the hours during which drink may be bought, but in the main the country is in a line with Warring- ton. That is why, at the meeting of the United Kingdom Alliance this week a resolution was carried declar- in n• that the Intoxicating Liquors (Temporary Restriction) Act was wholly .inadequate to meet the circum- stances of the time, and asking Par- liament to pass a general measure greatly curtailing the hours of open- ing of licensed houses and clubs, and Igiving the licensing authority in every district full discretionary powers. Sir Victor Horsley', the eminent doctor, who attended the meeting, made an onslaught upon the army of ' brewers and distillers "that hangs upon our flanks to rob us of our re- serves to the extent of 60,000 every year"; he also condemned the giving of rum to the soldiers at the front. "stuff," which, he said, "spells defeat for the British Arniy. It saps the energy of the' soldier, confuses his senses, spoils his shooting, but, above I all, destroys his morals and his dis- cipline." I A petition has been presented on 1 ' this subject of earlier closing in War- rington. What, may we ask, has become of it?

THE DREGS OF THE POPULATION.

Trade in Warrington has not been adversely affected by the war ; in fact, the demands of the Government for iron, wire, leather and rubber goods have made us busier than ever. So many men have gone to the war that there is a difficulty in meeting the orders, and much overtime has had to be worked. Men have been largely advertised for, and those who usually tramp from town to town, ostensibly , looking for work, have been stopped by the agents of some of the larger firms and offered jobs. Touting for labour is certainly a novelty, but des- perate needs require desperate means. Nin looking for work, and men who will work when they have found it,
