Others have made a start and succumbed, some in half a day, some in half a week, but very few have survived the ordeal of earning their living for a longer period. Here we have proof of the absolute need there is for declining to give alms indiscriminately. Able-bodied men who won't work in these times cer- tainly should not eat. We have better and more urgent need for our food-stuffs.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD SUGGESTION.

Many municipalities are asking the Government to allow them to suspend the repayment of their loans, and Warrington is one of these. No one would be the loser, the effect being that the repayments would fall on the community later, when the war is over. Present payment of interest would proceed. We in Warrington are now repaying loans to the amount' of £35,000 a year, and a great deal of good could be done with this amount during the period of the war. If relief works become necessary, which we all hope will not be the case, this sum would be available, but there are other expenses in view, such as the cost of making. Provision for War refugees on a large scale, in accord- ance with the desire of the Govern- ment. The Local Government Board have not yet ,sanctioned this suspen- sion of the Sinking Fund.

WOULD PLEASE THE KAISER. If the German Emperor could be informed of the scare a meteor caused in this part of the country on Tuesday night he would be highly satisfied with himself. A large part of his policy is to terrorise civilian populations, and we seem to be fairly well on the way to the state of nervousness that would please him when we frighten ourselves into the belief that bombs have been dropped here and there, because a light has been seen and a noise heard. In ordinary times these extraordinary revelations are seen about„ and just after "turning out time ''; but Tues- day's visitation took place about nine o'clock. As it was seen and heard in a large part of two counties at the same moment, it was clear that the airship theory that was promptly started could not be maintained. The scien- tific meh laid the ghost to rest, but there has been much gazing skywards since, and on Wednesday the writer saw groups peering at a large brilliant star, which they were trying to delude themselves into believing was swing- ing. With a little encouragement they would have made the light into an airship signal.

RELIEF FUNDS. The local contributions to the National Relief Fund is now within, a few pounds of £4,000. ' Over £7O has been contributed to the local war relief fund ; and besides substantial gifts to the Belgian Fund, over £lOO was raised by means of the Belgian tea on Thurs- day, the promoters of which are to be highly congratulated on the result.

WAR STORIES.

NNTARRINGTON QUINTET FIGHT SIDE BY SIDE. The men of the Royal Naval Brigade were among the last of the troops to leave Antwerp after the terrific bombardment, and a leading seaman serving with them, who is claimed by Warrington as a worthy son, yesterday gave a representative of " The Examiner" some details of the evacuation of the famous old Belgian cathedral city. Incidentally, he said: " There were five Warrington lads in my lot, and we-all stuck together. We fought shoulder to shoulder in the trenches, and we are now all together in Warrington. "Although I say it myself, 40,000 of us put up a good fight against 170,000 men, provided with tremendous siege guns, as they were. "There are some hard-hearted fellows in our brigade," said the interviewed, "but they dropped more than one tear as they saw the Belgian women and children clearing out of their homes."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EASY MATTER TO HIT THE

I

ENEMY: . Private T_lnstead, who belongs to Hailsham, Sussex, and who has been serving in the fighting line with the South Lancashires, is now in Orford Barracks, suffering from a wound in the head caused by a piece of shrapnel. He told our reporter that whilst he was lying uncon- scious on the ground one of the wheels of a British transport waggon ran over his left foot, and thus added to his injuries. "It was," he said, "a very easy matter to hit the enemy, as they came along like a swarm of bees."

ON THE AISNE. Interviewed by an "Examiner" man yester- day, a South Lanes. corporal, whose home is in the Bank Quay district, described his share in the Battle of the Aisne. "The German shells," he said, "came hurtling through the air play. ing tunes. When it came the turn of the Lan.- cashires to cross a bridge over the river at Vaillai the shells were falling so thickly that it resembled a hailstorm, so it was decided that we should not attempt the crossing that day. In the half-light of early dawn the following day, however, we again essayed to cross the bridge, and with the help of God we succeeded. "I was in charge of a number of men, and we got cut off from the rest," he continued. "Then Lieutenant Wadson came to us. He was a hero. We were making a bayonet charge, and he said, Push on,' lads!' As soon as_ the Germans saw us coming they ran away and screamed like women, but all at once a whistle sounded for us to cease firing, as the Getmans were going to surrender. We laid down wait- ing, and then some of our men -stood up again and began to walk towards the enemy, and as soon as they did so they were shot down. " Sergeant-Major Gasby, who was a War- rington man, was standing with a group of eight other men, when a shell burst in their midst and killed them all. We pushed on, and as we were preparing to charge, a bullet hit me on the hand and then went through my thigh. As I lay on the ground I heard our fellows cheering."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN WISDOM.

might the Supreme Right; Christianity Irrelevant.

A DELUDED PEOPLE.

BY C. E. PARKER.

The outbreak of war, and the part Germany played in it, has been a surprise to many. This surprise deepens into amazement as day after day we read of the brutality of the German Army, the violation of the humane rules laid down by the Hague Convention (to which Ger- many was a party), and the wanton destruction of many of the world's choicest treasures. It is only when we study the ideas which have prevailed in Germany during the last, say, 20 or 30 years that we can find the explanation of these strange happenings. Doubtless your readers have seen frequent references in recent speeches to such books as Bernhardi's and Pro- fessor Cramb's, and especially allusions to the great German philosophers, Nietzsche and Treitschke. We know that such works have been widely read and exercised a great influence over the German people. It may be useful, particularly to those who have not the oppor- tunity of perusing such books, to give the gist of their teaching. It is customary to blame Emperor William for this war, to think of him as a reckless autocrat,ic Ruler, who has dragged his country to the edge of an abyss and pushed them over; or, if the Emperor is not wholly blamed, the onus is put on a small group of Prussian Junkers, who form the Prussian military caste.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN PEOPLE'S EVIL CHOICE.

A perusal of the books named and a knowledge of the wide influence they have exerted, shows that the spirit we attribute to the Emperor and his immediate advisers is shared by a large and representative section of the German nation, so much so that we can almost speak of it as a national feeling. This reversion to barbariim is not the retrogressive of a few and the result of the war spirit; it is-the calculated and wil- ful choice of .a large section of the German people. They have chosen the worst and so sacrificed their national life. It is in Bernhardi's book, " Germany and the Next War," that we find the pleinest evidences of the spirit that brought about the European. cataclysm. Bernhardi is a reti.red German Cavalry General, and this book ap- peared about three years ago. He is the prac- tical man ,of war, and a good deal of his writ• ing is taken up with the military problems of the war which he sees looming ahead, the war which he considers inevitable, which if not begun by others, Germany must precipitate. There is little doubt the portions of the book which in any way can be considered philo- sophical is largely borrowed from the teaching of Treitschke. The following quotations from Bernhardi embody Treitschke's teaching. At least they do so according to this writer, al- though I fancy the philosopher if alive would demur to being commandeered to stlpport all that German militarism stands for:—

"We are accustomed to regard war as a curse, and refuse to recognise it as the greatest factor in the furtherance of culture and power." "German people are the greatest civilised people known to history." "We must not hold back in the hard struggle for the sovereignty of the world." "The man of strong will and intellect tries by every means to assert him Self, the ambi- tious strive to rise, and in this effort the individual is far from being guided merely by the consciousness of right." "Right is respected so far only as it is compatible with advantaga" In such cases .might gives the right to occupy or to conquer. Might is at once the supreme right, and the dispute as to what is right is decided by the arbitrament of war. War gives a biologically just decision, since its decisions rest on the very nature of things." " Love God above all things, and thy neighbour as thyself.' This law can claim no significance for the relations of one coun- try to another, since its application to Politics would lead to a conflict of duties. . . . Christian morality is personal and social, and in.its nature cannot be political." "The acts of the State cannot be judged by the standard of individual morality. . • • The raorality of the State must be judged by the nature and raison d'etre of the State, and not of the individual citizen. But the end-all and be-all of a State is power, and 'He who is not man enough to look this truth in the face should not meddle in politics.'"

WAR TO DECIDE. In spite of his repudiation of morality and Christian ethics as a guide in the conduct of nations, the writer entertains a strange idea about the influence of Germany on the progress of the world. , "A war fought and lost . . . would throw us back centuries, would shake the influence of German thought in the civilised world, and thus check the general progress of man- kind in its healthy development, for which a flourishing Germany is the essential condi- tion. Our next war will be fought for the highest interests of our country and of man- kind." Having thus settled to his own satisfaction that Germany must assume the sovereignty of the world, and this in the highest interest of man- kind, he proceeds to tell us how.this war should be brought about. "The greatness of true statesmanship con- sists in a knowledge of the natural trend of affairs, and in a just appreciation of the value of the controlling forces, Which it uses and guides in its own interest. It does not shrink,, from the conflicts, which under the iven con- ditions are unavoidable, but decides ;them resolutely by war when a favourable position affords prospect of a successful issue. In this way statecraft becomes a tool of Providence, 'which employs the human will to attain its . ends." Like the Kaiser, Bernhardi links Providence with the German cause. What conceptions he holds of the nature of what he calls Providence it is difficult to determine, but his God is cer- tainly not the All Father of the Christian Faith. Bernhardi tells with wonderful frankness the course the war is likely to take, and describes under what conditions it will be fought. There is a significant reference to Belgium's neutrality in this work. Why it is introduced is hot unless it is born of the idea at the balk 'of the writer's mind that the first move in the cam- paign should be that Germany *should strike at France through Belgium. Bernhardi's point is "That when Belgium was proclaimed neutral, no one contemplated that she would lay claim to a valuable region of Africa (Congo State)," and he says "That a State whose neutrality is guaranteed has no right to enter into political competition with the guaranteeing States." As to England respecting the neutrality of ' the small States (Denmark, Holland and Bel- gium) lie evidently has as low an opinion of this country as of his own.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREDIRLE THAT ENGLAND WOULD BE

HONEST.

He says "that En—gia-r-id- -Would pay much attention to the neutrality of weaker neigh- bours when stich a stake was at issue is hardly credible." It must be a surprise to him why we should at the moment faithfully respect Holland's neutrality by not making use of the Scheldt to reach our Allies' fortress of Antwerp. He tells us "the plan might be formed of anticipating the enemy by a sudden attack, instead of waiting passively for him to attack first, and of opening the war." With charac- teristic frankness he discusses the part England is likely to play in the next war. He says: "It is not unlikely that revolutions might break out in India and Egypt, if England's forces were long occupied with a European War. It is very questionable whether the English Army is capable of effectively acting on the offensive against Continental European troops." While acknowledging England's overwhelm- ing superiority on the sea, he says: "Our ships' cannons will probably show a superiority over the English, and our torpedo fleet, by its reck- less energy, excellent training, and daring spirit of adventUre, will make up some of the numerical disadvantage." Ridging England by his own standard, he thinks that we committed an unpardonable blunder (i.e., in our own (Continued in next column.)

OUR BELGIAJI GUESTS ARRIVE 7

War Victims are given Civic Welcome by the Mayor

===== 0002601_19141017_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORABLE SCENES AT THE 'aril TION AND TOWN HALL.

Sixteen Belgians arrived in Warrington on Wednesday evening, and are now comfortably quartered at St. Austin's House, Bold-street, which has been placed at the disposal of the local War Refugees Committee so generously by Mr. Robert Pierpoint, who witnessed their departure from London earlier in the day. The scene round about Bank Quay Station and on the platform was one which will be perma- nently imprinted in the minds of all who wit- , nessed it. A large number of Warrington's sons and daughters anxiously awaited the arrival of the 2.40 p.m. train from London with its homeless passengers. They took up their positions shortly before six o'clock, and several ladies and gentlemen had assembled on the platform along with the Mayor and Mayoress, members of the Council, and a number of those who con- stitute the War Refugees Committee. At about 6.25 the lights of the approaching train were seen, and as the train came to a standstill and it was ascertained that the party had travelled in the rear portion, those on the platform hurried forward. They were now a , silent, almost suffering, group, for were they not looking upon those who had lost everything they held dear defendinr, their country against the heartless braggart of Germany?

"There they are !"wa uttered in nothing more than a respectful whisper by almost every- one, as the Belgians alighted and became a pathetic little platform knot around their be- longings—insignificant in quantity or bulk, but very significant in meaning.

predation the English people had for the bravery and courage of the Belgians. Father Primavesi also addressed the gather- ing in French, and Mrs. Captain Bell spoke to the guests in Flemish. Councillor ROBERTS proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor for the opportunity he had given them of meeting their Belgian friends and the hospitality he had shown to them. (Hear, hear.) They all echoed the kind, earnest and sympathetic words the Mayor had addressed to the guests, and they would all do their best to make their friends happy and comfortable whilst they were in Warrington. It was pos- sible that arrangements would be made very shortly for more of their Belgian friends. (Hear, hear.) Three hearty cheers were given for the Mayor and Mayoress, and in reply the MAYOR said he had only done his duty, which he felt they all ought to do.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
STRICKEN BUT NOT DEJECTED.

Four or five small parcels Were all that these people had in the world. But did they appear as those who were there to welcome them ex-: pected? No doubt all were fearing a dejected, forlorn and pitiable batch, but they were pleas- urably disappointed. The men, women and children gave practical proof that a people who have suffered only as these have, know how to bear their troubles bravely and quietly. Their homes might have been burned to the ground, old land marks and pleasant haunts of youth might have been obliterated, but still they were undaunted, optimistic, and beautifully confi- dent. Perhaps they were additionally happy in the knowledge that they were in England, the home of liberty, and the land that has sol- emnly undertaken to help them to right their great wrong. The party consisted of a mother and father with four children, a mother and father with two children, a mother and father with one child, and two young wounded soldiers, who wore their army great coats. Miss Wilkinson, of Sunnyside, Latchford, and Father Primavesi acted as interpreters. After the Mayor and Mayoress had been intro- duced and had formally welcomed the guests, the civic bead, along with Alderman Bennett, who proudly shouldered a diminutive refugee, led the way from the station, and the party, smiling cordial thanks, passed through the blip of patient watchers below to waiting taxis, in which, by way of Bridge-street, they were con- veyed to the Town Hall, where a splendid high tea had been prepared for them. Cheer after cheer was given them as they mtered the taxis.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE TOWN HALL.

When the taxis arrived the Town Hall and the passengers made their way up the steps, led by Councillor Roberts with a baby in his arms, the people again cheered and again became awe- struck as one stately lady, deyoid of hat or coat, smiled a sweet smile of acknowledgment from tear-dimmed eyes. Amongst those who were gathered in the hall were the Mayor and Mayoress, Alderman Ben- nett, Councillors Roberts and Archer, Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Mr. J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Canon Willis, Fathers Primavesi and Tallon, Miss Wilkinson, Mrs. Henry Roberts, Mrs. V. B. Davies, Mrs. F. A. Frost, Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. Ralph Johnson, Mrs. Voisey, Mrs. Geary, Mine. Herzog, Mrs. Fair- hurst, Mrs. Carleton, Mrs. Pemberton, Mrs. John Evans, Miss Waring and Miss Broadbent. As the refugees filed into the Council Cham- ber, which had been tastefully decorated with flags of the Allies, they presented an impressive appearance to the sympathetic onlookers. They quite enjoyed the tea, over which. the Mayor presided; and seemed quite happy as they an- swered various questions put to them. Council- lor Roberts aptly put it to Our representative: "They seem much more cheerful than I ex- pected. They have had a little rest in London, and that has done them good. I think they look surprisingly well under the circumstances." It was ascertained that one of the soldiets, along with his wife and children, had been turned out of Malines, which place, he said, had been bombarded ten times. -Another, who was wounded, had come with his family from Ant- werp, whilst one male member of the party stated that he does not know where his wife's parents nor his four-year-old child are. His mother was in hospital at Ghent and he does not know what has become of her. The meal passed off successfully, very little difficulty having been experienced in ascertain- ing the needs of the guests. The refugees smiled their acknowledgment of any act of ser- vice with a grateful " Merci, Monsieur," or " Merci, Madamoiselle." ' j Mr. and Mrs. Mitchell had catered in excel- lent fashion, and had arranged the tables beautifully. •

===== 0002601_19141017_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S WELCOME.

In the course of his ;:peecliT'o-f—;;Tel.come, the MAYOR said: We are gathered here to welcome to our ancient borough our Belgian guests. I beg to offer them, in the name of Warrington, our warmest hospitality. I can assure them that during their stay we shall do all we can to make them comfortable and happy. (Hear, hear.) We in England owe a great 'deal to Bel- gium in defending their beautiful country so gallantly and so brilliantly, and stopping the rush of the Prussian murderers. Such vitality, heroism' and sacrifice on the part of our friends deserves not only the appreciation of England, but of all the civilised world. I little thought a few months ago, when I, along with Mayors of various other boroughs in England, enjoyed the hospitality of the Brussels Cor- poration, that our excellent friends in Belgium would have to come to the position they have. Little did I think when they tried to outdo each other in making the English Mayors very wel- come that it would be my lot, unfortunately, to-night to entertain some of the Belgian people in this Town Hall. I shall never forget the generous way in which the Belgian %people ' treated us when we visited them officially, and I feel quite sure I am voicing the feelings of the whole of Warrington when I say that we shall feel no sacrifice too great to end this cruel and vile tyranny of German arrogance. (Hear, hear.) We welcome you to Warrington. We are sorry you have had to come here, but while you are here we shall give you a right down English welcome in gratitude of the service you have done for us. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Robert Pierpoint has wired from London stating that his wife is ill and cannot come here. You will stay in Mr. Pierpoint's house, which is fur- nished very nicely, and I hope you will be very comfortable. I take this first public oppor- tunity of thanking the Ladies' Committee who haVe worked so well to make the quarters so very nice and comfortable. I am sorry Mr. Pierpoint is not here, but I am sure you will be happy in your new surroundings. (Ap- plause.) Miss Wilkinson conveyed the Mayor's speech to the Belgians in French, and spoke of the ap-

GERMAN WlSDOM—continued. interest) in not supporting the Southern States' of America in the Civil War, because by allow- ing North America to become united we allowed a powerfu) rival to our world-wide Empire to he built ulp in the West. , In this, as indeed all through the book, the outlook is altogether low and sordid, his German culture grovels on the earth, and he ascribes to other nations motives as unworthy as those of the land for which he speaks. The Germans having repudiated all respect for international morality, have lost their power of appreciating the motives of the nations whose respect for this is steadily on the increase.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW " HOME."

One of the refugees briefly returned thanks to their host, and then the party made their way to St. Austin's House, where everything had been prepared for the guests. The rooms have been arranged with artistic taste, the piano and other pieces of furniture being very handsome—a striking contrast to the smoking ruins in Belgium. There is a nursery for the children, with a beautiful large rocking horse, and this room will be used as a smoke- room for the men dur;ng the evening. The Belgians will, without doubt, find the rest, quiet and content, for which they have been longing, within these walls.

BELGIANS INTERVIEWED. 'Charred Remains in Fire-witted

Homes.

Yesterday an "Examiner" reporter paid a visit to St. Austin's House, and had brief in- terviews with one or two of the guests to whom Warrington is proud and pleased to be able to accord shelter and hospitality in these troub- lous days. He found the whole of the party in wonderfully good spirits considering, and full of appreciation and gratitude for what is being done to lessen the sore burden that the war has imposed upon them. The Belgians are a nice' noted for their independence of spirit and every; ,naiss has been taken in housing and catering for; these poor people, to let them feel that such kindness as we are able to show to them is not extended out of mere charity, but from a sense of duty, and as a poor, but well-intentioned, return for the gallant services the little nation, to which they have the hon- our of belonging, has rendered to her bigger brothers in this most terrible of all wars.

TOYS FOR THE TINY TOTS. , Everything possible is being done foi the comfort of the refugees, and to help them to forget the hardships they have undergone, the perils they have passed through, and the losses they have suffered. Pipes and tobacco have been supplied to the men, and it would glad- den the heart of the donors to see the relish with which the men puff away, whilst the women are no less delighted with the more feminine comforts supplied by kindly-hearted friends, and they exhibit with pride and evi- dent joy the fal-lals and frills—so dear to the heart of womenkind all the world over—which have fallen to their lot from the big pile of clothes and fripperies contributed from the boudoirs' of the town. The children have not been forgotten. Toys, old and new, have been showered upon them, and the special favourite is a big rocking-horse, whieti is so overworked that were it of flesh and blood the R.S.P.C.A. would inevitably be called upon to intervene. To see the smiling faces of the Belgian tots and hear their happy, care-free laughter, one would hardly imagine that they had so lately been within the sound of the death-dealing guns, and had gazed with their sparkling young eyes upon the wastage and ruin of war.

GRATEFUL GUESTS. One of the refugees with whom our represen- tative—aided by the kindly offices of the Rev. ' Father Primavesi, as interpreter and prompter —had a conversation, was loud in his gratitude of "Les Anglais"—he would never forget it; it was magnificent. Asked to speak of himself, he gave his name as Tbeophil Rens, and said that prior to the war he followed the occupa- tion of a boatman. He was in Termonde when the historic engagement in the neighbourhood of that town took place. The population had to take to their heels—leaving everything ex- cept the most easily,- portable of their chattels behind them—and make for Louvain as quickly as possible. But it was only a case of "jump- ing out of the frying pan into the fire," for soon after he had found shelter and succour in "the Oxford of Belgium," the town was bom- barded, and as many of the people as possible fled elsewhere. Every man able to bear arms was called upon to aid, and M. Rens was not slow in offering his services. The major under whom he was acting was killed during the fighting, which raged all night, and continued well into the next day. At daybreak the Bel- gians were compelled to fight a fierce rear- guard action, and, harassed all the way, they eventually reached Malines.

FIERCE FIGHTING IN A WOOD. The battle that took place in the neighbour- hood of that town, M. Hens described as awful —too awful for words, he insisted, with an expressive shrug of the shoulders and an up- lifting of his hands. Nevertheless, he was induced to give a few particulars. The battle, he said, raged all round the town. The Ger- mans kept coming and going—now advancing, now retiring. For hours the battle swayed backwards and forwards, and for a long time the plucky Belgian riflemen forced the Ger- mans to seek the shelter of a neighbouring wood, from which, though many times outnum- bering the defenders, they did not dare to come Out until considerable reinforcements came up. Meanwhile, from the thickets the huge German guns poured out death and destruction. When, eventually, the Germans did emerge, they were soon driven back again, and eventually the order was given to follow them among the trees and clear them out at all costs. The task was a terrific one, and M. Rens said he would never forget the terrible charge across the open land that fringed the outlying thickets. The Bel- gians suffered terribly, but they accomplished their end, and for a long time they held the wood in spite of repeated attacks, alternating with terrific gun fire. Eventually, however, they were forced to retreat within their own lines. For several days the battle was waged in the neighbourhood of the wood, and even- tually, when it was discovered that the Ger- mans had got their heavy siege guns into posi- tion, and were beginning to bombard the city, it was deemed advisable to withdraw.

KING AND HIS COMRADES IN ARMS. The next fight in which M. Rens was engaged was in the neighbourhood of Wierde, and he

relates, with pride, how that in that battlete the King of the Belgians was Preeellt' eafnal thati on one side of him rode a Fren IP ch GeT„,,er a 1 nd on the other side a British General. Infectious Disc.ase Prevalent. Last week the following cases of infection's diseases in Warrington were reported :—Scarlet fever, 35; diphtheria, 3; tuberculous phthisis, 13; erysipelas, 3. There were 125 patients in the hospital.:—Scarlet fever. 92; enteric fever, 1; diphtheria, 11; tuberculoski, 21.

Church Society.

Last night a service under the auspices of the Warrington Church Society was held in the parish Church. The Rev. A. E. Cornibere, of Salford, preached from the text: "I was not disobedient unto the heavenly vision."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
How IVe Die.

According to the weekly report of the Medical Officer of Health (Dr. G. W. N. Joseph) there were last week 29 deaths from the following causes in Warrington :—Scarlet fever, 3; enteric fever, 2; diarrhoea, 4; enteritis, 1; phthisis, 2; cancer, 4; bronchitis, 2; pneumonia, 1; heart disease, 2; all other causes, 8.

Death of Mr. James Ford. We regret to announce the death of Mr. Jas. Ford, which occurred on Thursday, at his residence, Pinewood-avenue. Mr. Ford had a life-long connection with Warrington Metho- dism, and in earlier life served the churches of the circuit as a preacher. He was in his 83rd year. The funeral takes place to-day at 2.45, at the Warrington Cemetery.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
The Picture House.

- - The Picture House, Winmarleigh-street, has been taken over by the management of the Picturedrorue. It has been re-decorated and considerably altered inside, and is certainly now one of the leading places of entertainment in the town. The experience which has been the foundation of the success of the Picture- drome is now available for the Picture House, and will guarantee its efficiency. A substan- tial proportion of the takings are being set aside for the provision of comforts for the soldiers.

Drowned in the Mersey. "Ile had a son killed at the Forge three years ago, and that seemed to have an effect on his mind." Such was a statement made at the inquest, held by Mr. F. A. Jones at War- rington on Wednesday, on. the body of Thomas Burns (56), who resided at 4, Stanley-street.--- It was also stated that' deceased had been mis- sing for a week, and that his body was recov- ered from the River Mersey on Monday.—A verdict of "Found drowned" was returned.

Masoiilc Installation.

At the annual festival of the " Gilbert Greenall" Lodge, held on Tuesday at the Lion Hotel; the banquet was dispensed with owing to the war. The W.M.-elect, Bro. T. C. Wadsworth, was installed Worshipful Master by W. Bro. Thomas A.G.D.C., and invested his offi- cers as follows :—W. Hainswoiih, 1.P.M.; 11. Maddock, S.W.; H. Taylor, J•W.; E. Rose, P.M., P.P.D.G.D.C., secretary; T. Hill, treasurer; Rev. B. Percival, chaplain; H. Collin, S.D.; T. Reddish, J.D.•' E. Allen, 1.G.; A. C. Binks, steward; J. Gray, steward; H. Generous Shepherds. The quarterly meeting of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds kA.U.), Warrington Dis. _____ „- . .

_ trict, was held on Saturday at the "fate Spring Rose" Lodge, Barley Mow, Warrington. Prov. C. S. Francis Taylor presided. It was decided to pay the contributions of all those members of the society who were serving their country. The meeting agreed to a levy for this purpose. P.M. John l'arr was elected Banner Master, pro. tem., owing to Banner Master Taylor hav- ing joined the Territorials. At the close of the meeting Immediate P.P.C.S. W. E. Clegg was presented with a gold watch for services ren- dered to the district as Prov. Chief Shepherd during 1913-14. P.P.C.S. J. W. Pomfrey made the presentation, and Bro. Clegg suitably responded.

Funeral of the Late Mr. Ben. Grimshaw Mr. 'Benjamin Grimshaw, of Gate Wharf Farm, Sankey, whose serious illness was an- nounced in our last issue, passed away on Tuesday morning, at the age of 70 years. The funeral service was conducted by the Rev. Jones, of Sankey, and the interment took place at the Warrington Cemetery yesterday after- noon. Among those who attended the funeral were: Mr. Brookfield, Mr. Renshaw Beswick, Mr. Gray, Mr. Samuel Beswick, Mr. Henry Bes- wick, Mr. E. S. Owen, Mr. T. Plant, Mr. Harry Grinishaw, Mr. Enoch Sankey, Mr. George Pilling, Mr. James Ditchfield, Dr. Bennett, Mr. Leonard Bennett, Mr. John Cross, Mr. John Leigh, Mr. and Mrs.. James Houghton, Coun- cillor W. H. J. Jenkins, Mr. Cooper, Mr, Shearer, Mr. Thomas Perk, Mi. George Pinch, Mr. Charles Kilshaw, Mr. T. Warhurst, Mr. T. Kilshaw, Mr. Albert Denham, Mr. J. Rough- ley, Mr. G. Lester. Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons carried out the funeral arrangements.

Another 'Bus Fatality.

_ Mr. F. ATJones held an inquest, at War- rington yesterday, on the body of Phillip Ireland (5), the son of John Phillip Ireland, a general labourer, of 77, Ilaydock-street, who was run over and faitally injured by a Corpora- tion motor 'bus on Wednesday.—John Edward Bolton, motorman, employed by the Corpora- tion, said that he was driving the 'bus down the middle of the road by ' Central. Station, because there was a steam lurry on one side and a horse 'bus on the other. He had his brakes half on, but, as he passed the horse 'bus, the boy rushed out from the side and was run over. He died in the Infirmary the fol- lowing morning.—John Walter Boswell a cab- driver, of 97, Lythgoe's-lane, said the driver had no chance of avoiding the accident as the boy darted out so suddenly.—A verdict of "Acci- dental death" was returned, and the jury expressed their sympathy with the relatives, as also did Mr. Hallaway for the Corporation, the latter adding that although the evidence did not show there was any liability on them, the Corporation would be prepared to deal sym- pathetically with the parents.—The father thanked the jury and Mr. Hallaway, and re- marked on the kindness of the Corporation.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
Wounded Warrior at P.S.A.

- - --------- The claims of Dr. Barnardo's Homes were urged at the usual pleasant afternoon service , in Wycliffe Church on Sunday. Mr. Davies, the Manchester agent for the Homes, attended and explained the work that was being done for homeless and orphaned children. Teeming thousands, he said, had passed through the Homes, and, in the great majority of cases, had , been made into good citizens. No less than 98 , per cent, of the children sent from the Homes to Canada had done well. The Homes had ever- open doors—no destitute child was refused. The children were taken in first and the inquiries were made afterwards. Every town in the land sent their unprotected children to the Homes, knowing that once they were there they would be well cared for; that they would re- ceive a good education and would learn useful trades, which would fit them to play their parts in the battle of life. They were not only cared for whilst they were of tender age and lived in ' the homes, but afterwards; in fact, they were watched over and helped until they were 21. , The Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) added a few words in support of the claims of the Homes, and went on to voice the sympathy of the local P.S.A. Society with Mr. Jos. Garner, the death of whose wife recently occurred after a long illness.—The Rev. T. T. James mentioned, with pleasure, the fact that Private Yeates, .of Orford, who was wounded in the Battle of the Aisne, was so far recovered as to be able to attend that afternoon. The reverend gentle- man also referred to the presence of Private Cousins, who was, he said, quartered in Orford Barracks, and who belonged to a P.S.A. in •Penarth, where his father was an active Chris- tian worker. During the afternoon Mr. A. Davies sang "Nazareth." About 110 prize books were distributed and the sum of 30s. was • collected for Barnardo's Homes.

EXAMINER, S ATURDAY, OCTOBER 17, 1914.

CALL TO ARMS' War Stories: Bu Letter & Interview

Young Warrington Terrier's "Secret"

For His Mother.

THE BATTALION'S GOOD NAME.

I am writing home specially to let you all into my secret. . . . I am glad to hear there are plenty of young chaps joining the "Ter- riers," because we need them, and I am very glad to hear that B— has joined. . • I am going to stick to them all my life, because I have learnt that they need me very much, although I am only a young lad. If I had anything to do with it, I would make every- body join between the ages of 16 and 40. . • • Now my secret. It is, 1 have joined for foreign service. I joined not only for the sake of going on foreign service, but to help the Bat- talion to keep its good name. Just fancy now, of half of us did not join, after us all volun- teering to go, what would the Warrington people think of us, and all the other Batta- lions? Why, they would sneer at us for all our lives, and that is worse than torture; and, as long as I am doing my duty, anybody, barring Germans, may say what they like and do what they like.

WALTER. —From a letter written to his mother by one of the youngest of our Territorials now in camp.

THE WEEK'S RECRUITING. The recruits towards the 700 met needed to complete the reserve battalion of the Terri- torials now number 320, so that 380 are still re- quired. Eighty have joined this week. About 100 men have joined the Regular Army at Bearnont School and the Police Station. Men from the larger Warrington works con- tinue to swell the ranks. This week about 50 have enlisted—the same number as last week. Messrs. Rylands' figure-10—wa5 the highest this week, the St. Helens Cable Company gave six men, whilst Messrs. Crosfield'O contributed four.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALLS FOR "MR. ATKINS." Much Appreciated Gifts from the Warrington Club.

A few days ago the committee of the War- rington Footbalf Club, realising that even in the serious days of war some members of the huge and ever-growing family of Atkins may find a few minutes in which to indulge in the health-giving pastime so popular in the piping' times of peace, forwarded two footballs, and a supply of pants and jerseys, to the quarters of the 'Territorials and also of the recruits of the South Lancashire Regiment. In acknowledgment three interesting letters have been received by the Secretary (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw). The first came from Colonel Brereton Fair- dough, of the Territorials, who, after expressing thanks to the Club Committee, wrote: "I am issuing one football to each half-battalion, and a proportion of the pants, &c." Referring to the heartfelt good wishes of the club for the safety of the recruits and of the officers, the Colonel said: "I heartily appreciate them and will convey the same to the Battalion." Lieutenant-Colonel Malcolm Green, of the South Lancashire Regiment, also forwarded a letter of thanks, in. which he states that the gifts are very much appreciated, and heartily reciprocates the kind wishes of the Club Com- mittee. "All ranks," he adds, "are doing their very best to make themselves fit to uphold in the face of the enemy the good name of the South Lancashires and of the town of War- rington."

A- third letter was from Lieutenant H. 11. Withers, of the South Lancashires, who wrote: "We are most grateful for the balls and jerseys. They will be a help to us. Will you please con- vey our thanks to the members of the club for their good wishes, which we reciprocate most heartily?"

===== 0002601_19141017_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUR FIGHTING FUSILIERS. Warrington Family's Contribution to the Ranks.

Mr. Edward Sykes, of Warrington, has not only rendered good industrial service to the community, having for close upon half a. cen- tury been employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros.', Church-street works, but he has also given four of his sons—who have been augmented by a son- in-law—to the service of their country. One of the sons—a reservist--comparatively recently answered the call to rejoin the colours, and is at present -fighting with the Army in France. A second is acting as sergeant-instructor to a Territorial Battalion, whilst a third is serving as a lance-corporal in India. A fourth has re- enlisted in Lord Kitchener's Service Battalion, in which, by virtue of previous experience, he holds the rank of pioneer-sergeant. All four brothers are attached to the Northumberland Fusiliers. he son-in-law is an engine-room artificer in the Royal Navy. All of Mr. Sykes' soldier sons were born in Warrington, and the old town will join him in feeling very proud of them.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR RESOURCES.

Why We Must Win in the Long Run.

:we must win, said Lord- Haldane at New- castle, if we only have the qualities—which I believe we have—of spirit, courage, resolution, and dogged determination. Germany may be admirably prepared, but preparations wear off against people who have the resources that we have. Germany has a population of some 65 millions and Austria about 50 millions-115 millions in all. But we have 44 millions, France 38 millions, and Russia has over 170 millions. Therefore, we have 250 millions in population against 115 millions. Then, as to armies, we will put the German Army,. say, at six mil- lions, but Russia can mobilise six million men , and more. Say Austria can mobilise two mil- lion men. We and France can mobilise be- tween three and four million men between us. 'Even in troops there is a marked superiority in our numbers. Then ,there is the splendid fighting of Belgium. In resources the Allies , have double the wealth and reserves to draw upon. Ido not underrate the magnitude of the task that lies before vs, but I trust the shock ' is over and the real struggle is now beginning under much less favourable circumstances for Germany than was the case two months ago. Set your teeth and stick it. Up to now the resolution of the nation has been splendid, and the signs are that it is increasing and not dim- inishing. We have shown that we can fight in a manner worthy of the 'example of our ances- tors.

LOCAL DOCTOR FOR THE FRONT. A commission in the Royal Army Medical Corps was recently obtained by Dr. H. Nield, son of the late Councillor and Mrs. Nield, of Latchford. Dr. Nield has now sailed from Southampton for the front.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON NAVAL MEN IN ANTWERP. First-hand Description of the Fight and Evacuation.

Thrilling adventures are narrated by the British Naval men just returned from Antwerp, and in these the treachery of the German spies plays a large part. Mr. Frank Wilson has this week returned to his home, 21, Willis-street, Warrington, in company with Mr. Frank Moore, a Birkenhead friend. They are both members of the First Brigade of the Naval Volunteers Division, the former having joined at the begin- ning of the war. Training was commenced, says Mr. Wilson, down at Betteshangar, in Kent. Some time after we went for a three days' march from Bet- teshangar to Chatham, where we were to take musketry training. We had got about half-way there when orders to turn back to Dover were received, and from there we sailed for Dunkirk. Of course, we exPected we were going to finish training there, and we were very much surprised when we were sent straight into the firing line, together with 11,000 of the Naval Brigade and Marines.

_ But to get back to the story. We left the boat at Dunkirk on Sunday, September 27th, and en- trained for Antwerp.

A ROYAL RECEPTION.

Here we had a royal reception; the people could not do enough for us. They gave us all sorts of cigars and cigarettes, and if any wanted drinks they could have as many as they liked. In fact, they would have given us nearly every- thing they possessed, they were so glad to see us. Well, we were put into a big winter garden place and told that we would be billeted there for the night; but after we had been in there half an hour, we were called up, and sent straight into the firing line, and the fun began. We proceeded to make sheltered trenches for ourselves by digging a hole in the ground, about four feet deep, and covering it with wood and earth till it looked just like the ordinary road, and we planted bushes all along in front, in ease hostile airmen flew overhead looking for us; the bushes, of course, gave the place quite a natural appearance.

IN THE TRENCHES BEFORE ANTWERP. At last we got the whole trench fixed pro- perly, whereupon we were ordered to move to a more suitable position. Marching along about four miles, we took up a place on the right wing of the defence, right in the firing line, just as day was breaking. Here the Belgian, soldiers had already made the trenches shrapnel-proof, and for a day we remained in them, nothing particular happening, except the loud and con- tinual bursting of the German shells overhead, which prevented us getting any sleep whalso- ever. Towards night the Germans began to send out patrols, and, as they kept coming-into sight, each of us fired about four rounds at them. They only attacked us in the night time, for their aeroplanes were out scouting for our positions during the day. • Of course, our rifles were noth- ing compared with their big guns; we could not get nearly the range that they could. Leaving our sentries on guard, we at length went back to the trenches; but it was quite impossible to sleep for the noise of the shells— we only got about a quarter of an hour's nap that night! For three days and nights we remained in the trenches, during which time we fed on hard biscuits and bully-beef. There were not many biscuits, but we did not mind very much, for they were hard to eat—in fact, I offered one to a dog on the way home, and it wouldn't touch it! But sleep we really did miss, and the shells went on bursting all round for three days, we firing on the German patrols each night. By that time we had managed to obtain more provisions, and we were just preparing for a good "feed," when the order came for us to retreat.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
A " BELGIAN " OFFICER.

Phei.v ! At six o'clock the same night we started off, led by one of the Belgian officers. It was a great strain, marching like that after four days and nights, during which we had never had more than 15 minutes' sleep at a time. After marching five miles we were chal- lenged by a Belgian sentry, who informed us that we were walking right into a complete regiment of German cavalry! Our officers were naturally amazed, so to discover that our guide was not a Belgian at all, but a treacher- ous German spy! 110 was promptly dealt with as the officers thought necessary. We naval men, of course,. were not supposed to know anything about this, but somehow the truth leaked out and went round the brigade. Well, back we marched practically the same way we had come, and went on to St. ,Gilles. On the way we passed blazing oil fields, the terrific heat from which nearly scorched us as we went past.

MILES OF REFUGEES. Along the road there passed miles and miles of refugees—an incessant stream, of which we neither saw the start or finish. They had bundles of belongings' but no food; and they were shivering so with the cold, that, altogether, the brigade gave them over eight thousand of our own blankets, as they lay along the road- side. We drew near Antwerp again at length, and crossed the River Scheldt by means of a pon- toon bridge, which had been constructed by some of the Engineers. The last man had been crossed only a quarter of an hour, when the bridge was blown to smithereens, presum- ably by German spies. But, then, the whole country is absolutely infested with spies, even in the most unexpected places! Antwerp! It was in a terrible state! When we had been there at first it was a most beau- tiful city, full ,of fine buildings and, lovely scenes. TheSe were gone now; the populace had fled. It was doomed to fall into the hands of barbarism! We continued marching until six o'clock the following morning, covering a distance of about 32 miles. The strictest discipline was observed throughout this march, we not being allowed even to speak, in case the sound might betray us to hostile forces in the vicinity. Arriving at St. Gilles, we entrained for Bruges, but the engine driver and stoker re- fused to drive the train.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
ARRIVAL AT BRUGES.

However, two of our own men, who were engine drivers in ordinary life, volunteered to run it instead, and so we arrived at Bruges. We then proceeded to the Town Hall, where we had a good meal of soup and bread; and, I can tell you, we were very thankful for it I Afterwards, sleeping quarters were provided for us in schools, and we had a good night's rest. All our kits had been left at one of the stations for safety, and we now found that the whole station had been burnt down, so the only thing to do was to go back to England for a week, during which time we could procure new out- fits. So the following day, after a lengthy wait on the Bruges station, we caught a train to Ostend, where we slept all night on a ship, embarking the next day for Dover. Upon arrivaMhere, my friend and I came here, home.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME AGAIN.

Home! That night we lay awake for two hours, trying to realise that we were in real beds! I had not been in one since the begin- ning of the war, almost three months before— in fact, as I said, none of us had more than 15 minutes' sleep at a time from our arrival on the Continent to our rest at Bruges; neither had I had a shave for a fortnight, nor a wash for a week! We were very glad of a change of clothes when we got home again. But, concluded Mr. Wilson, we shall be still gladder when we are back at the front again!

===== 0002601_19141017_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN WOLVES.

Private Kinsella (King's Liverpool Regiment) writing to a friend at Widnes after the Battle of the Aisne, says : lam lucky to be alive. I was leaning against a tree with my two chums of our company when a German shell burst about ten yards away and killed both of them. A piece of the 'shell struck me on the head, and my rifle was blown to bits.

It is cruel to see all the homes here ruined and the homeless women and children. The Germans are like a gang of ravenous wolves, and they have the stains of children's blood on them. I saw a small boy and a woman with a baby in her arms. The boy was dead, and his innocent face made me weep. The mother and the baby were wounded. We laid the boy to rest with Dome of our men we were burying.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
BELGIAN LADY AT STOCKTON

HEATH. Husband's Terrible Suspense in Brussels.

The following letter is from a Belgian resi- dent in Brussels who is the business represen- tative in that city of one of the large manufac- turing concerns in Warrington. His wife—now the guest of a family living at Stockton Heath —came over here about a month ago, and, such are the painful exigencies of the war, that she is likely to remain in her present hospitable shelter for an indefinite period—a refugee who has fled from the dire conditions at present pre- vailing in her unhappy country. The letter, which is addressed to the host with whom the lady is living, is so eloquent in its human appeal and in the expression of gratitude for succour rendered to one held dear, that we make no apology for publishing it at the present juncture. Dear Mr.

Brussels, Oct. 4, 1914.

I hope that you have received my former letters, but it appears that several couriers who set out from Brussels for Ostend have been stopped, and it would not be astonishing if several of my letters have gone astray. The present letter will be posted in London by an American leaving for his country, and going by England. At Brussels life is becoming Very enervating. For six days we have heard cannon day and night without ceasing. Au! it is terrifying. and to think that each report takes away several human lives; parents, friends, acquaintances are perhaps massacred each instant—it is enough to make one mad to think of it!

Is -then this frightful war the recompense of our poor little country, so courageous, so indus- trious, and, above all, so beautiful—now trans- formed into a pile of ruins; everywhere it is death and desolation! How we curse the being who is the cause of all these horrors, and hope that his punishment will be soon. Hearing all day cannon, not knowing where the action is taking place, having no details of the result of the battle, whether we are conquerors or con- quered, we know absolutely nothing; we live as if in an immense prison, completely isolated from the rest of the world. This life is terrible! And how long will it last? God alone knows! And with that we look upon retreat; what will happen then? Will our beautiful town of Brussels be respected, or will it share the fate of Louvain, Dinant, Aerschot, Rheims, Charleri, Mons., &c., and what will happen to us? The bad season comes, and food commences to , fail; bread is scarce, and above all bad; more coffee, sugar, soap, coal, the most necessary things, are wanting. In fine, the privations will , be, perhaps, hard, but we will bear them as well as possible, and have an absolute confidence , in the final success, and we place great hope on your English Army, and above all in your finan- cial assistance, thanks to which we hope to see re-established our little country, so cruelly tried.

If I were not married I would be with my brothers at the front of battle and try to make the injury done to our land cost dear, but I have to remember that I have the charge of a soul, and my existence is necessary to my wife. I cannot express to you all that I feel, for one must be prudent and measure his words. We hope soon that .our situation will change and that .I will be able to come for my wife. I hope that she is less sad now; it is very un- fortunate for her to have to be separated from the one she cherishes most in the world. _ _

I know that Mrs. and yourself do all you can to cheer up my wife, and I thank you very sincerely, and you may be certain that I wilL always be grateful. With kind regards to Mrs. —, and hoping to see you soon,

Sincerely yours.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
FORCING THE GERMANS BACK. AVarrior Who Was Wounded in "the

Great Drive."

Private Campbell, of Londonderry, one of the South La.ncashires, at present rests in the War- rington Barracks Hospital, after having served for six weeks with the Army in France, where he received a wound in the jaw. The detach- ment to which he belongs went out to reinforce the troops returning from the Battle of Mons, and, after meeting them, proceeded to drive back the Germans, from trench to trench, until the Aisne was reached. To his regret, how- ever, circumstances prevented the gallant pri- vate participating in the actual battle at that river. One of a force of six hundred, he had been marching forward for a whole day and night, when he fell into a German ambush. The enemy were concealed on either side of the road, and immediately opened a heavy rifle fire on the entrapped men.

Shot Through the Shoulder. A shot passed clean through the shoulder of the " Tommy " in front of Private Campbell, and struck the latter in the jaw. Inflammation 'afterwards set in, and it was necessary to send him home, and he was included in a nuMber of British soldiers, who were escorting a de- tachment of German prisoners over to England. Upon arrival at Southampton, he journeyed to, and entered a hospital in the South, afterwards being transferred to Warrington. lie expects to return to France shortly. The many scenes of devastation—ruined homes, gutted churches, sacked towns—which met the eye on all sides, were described to our representative by Private Campbell as "terrible," whilst, he added, it was particu- larly pathetic to see the distracted women and children wandering about the wasted country- side.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
INJURED IN RETREAT FROM MONS.

Corporal Cleave. Upset by Bursting Shell.

Corporal Frederick Charles Gleave, the youngest son of Mr. William Gleave, 12, Paul- street, Warrington, who enlisted during the Boer War, and has since been in the Reserve, was one of the first to go to the front in the present war, which lie did in the capacity of a driver in the Royal Field Artillery. He has now been invalided home.

Writing to his father, from the hospital at Winchester, Corporal Gleave declares that Ile is not sorry to be back in Old England. "Pour days after landing in France," he continues, "we went into action, and it was simply hell upon earth. The bullets were flying around like hailstones, but I was not hit. The sixth day of the retreat I came a cropper. A shell burst under my horse, and I came down. I could not get clear from my horse, and my left shoulder and a couple of ribs were broken. An old chum of mine-went aloft the first day. We lost our Colonel and Adjutant, and all the offi- cers were wounded. The French retreated in the night, not knowing anything about it. We stuck it as best we could, and saved all our • guns. _ _

- "lam to have a couple of days' leave. My arm is now free, but will never be straight again, owing to being without treatment for three days. I only have the clothes, at present, I went away with, and am looking forward to a change. I had a shave to-day for the first time—l had grown at beard ;something like yours! lam as fat as a bullock; we cannot eat all that is supplied. We are staying at present at Sir G. Cooper's, who has placed his grounds at the disposal of the Government. We get everything we want, but you ought to see the poor fellows without arms or legs. I never hear a grumble. It is all luck." When Corporal Gleave resumes duty, he is to go on the staff at the Southampton base, as a clerk.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
IS THERE TO BE A TRUCE?

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—l do not wish at this critical position in the history of our country to start a political controversy, but perhaps it would be as well if we understand what action the political parties intend to take in the forthcoming municipal elections. I believe the Liberals and Conservatives have already declared a truce. The Socialists have, I believe, been asked to co-operate in this good work, but up to the present they have not done anything that gives one the impression that they are in favour of presenting a united front at this time.

On the contrary, they have shown direct hos- tility to such a course, and are nominating three candidates for the forthcoming November elec- tions. If that is the intention of the Socialist party, I should like to ask why have they chosen this year as the most suitable time for their intervention? Is it because they have not had the opportunity before, or because they feel that now that people have their attention riveted on the war it is a suitable time for their propaganda? or is it because they feel that now that so many Liberals and Tories are winning victories at the front they have a chance of winning municipal elections at hmie? If they go forward with these contests they will put the town to an expense that it can ill- afford at this time, when all our funds should be reserved to meet the terrible cost of the war. If these elections are forced upon us, the re- sponsibility will rest entirely with the Social- ists, and it is only right the people should know that the politicaZ organisations which have become Red Cross societies will have to take up the work of electioneering for at -least a fortnight, when they could ..)th...rwise have been helping our soldiers and sailors at the front. If the Socialists of Warrington really desire to help the unfortunate, and put their loudly professed sympathy with the down-trodden and weak into practice, they cannot do better than think about Louvain, and help the Belgian refugee, and leave personal ambition and expe- diency in municipal matters to look after them- selves for the present. VINCIT AMOR PATRI.E. October 15th, 1914.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
NO TRUCE.

Labour to Fight in Three Wards. DISAFFECTED INSURANCE MEN.

After all, there is to be no "peace in Novem- ber," for, at their meeting on Tuesday evening, the delegates to the Warrington Trades and Labour Council decided to lend the support of that body to the candidature for municipal honours of Mr. Charles Dukes (the secretary of the Council) for the Orford Warct; of Mr. A. E. Seabury for the Bewsey Ward; and of Mr. J. Helsby for the St. John's Ward. This decision was arrived at in camera, and was communicated to the Pressmen at the close of a long meeting. Prior to this decision being arrived at, it was reported, in open meeting, that the local branch of the National Union of Life Assur- ance Agents had decided to cease their affilia- tion with the Council. Mr. Heywood (a delegate of the Union in question) said be considered the branch had done wrong, and he was surprised at their action. The branch had a membership of seventy or eighty, and only about twenty were , present at the meeting at which secession was I decided upon, whilst even then the resolution announced in the letter was only carried by eight votes to five. . Mr. J. Helsby (another delegate from the ' same Union) said the action had been taken from purely political motives. When it was announced in the papers that his name was for municipal honours, quite a stir was caused among the members of the Union, who were not in the habit of attending the branch meet- ings. The fact that he was to fight a Conserva- tive was a very grievous matter to them, and accordingly those who hardly ever came near a meeting took the trouble to turn up at the branch meeting last Friday week to register a protest against his candidature going forward.

The Protest Too Late.

He (the speaker) was also at that meeting, and he told the protesters that they were toe

No , a

late, as the matter was then in the hands of the Trades Council. Thereupon they left the meeting in a body, but they turned up at a subsequent branch meeting last Friday, bring- ing a few sympathisers with them, and moved that the branch cease its affiliation with the Trades Council. Those in favour of remain- ing affiliated put up as good a fight as pos- sible, but the majority had evidently come de- termined to put a stumbling block in the way of the contest. To test the motives of the movers of the proposal he (the speaker) put forward an amendment to the effect that the matter should be deferred to the annual meet- ing of the branch in January, but it was de- feated.

THE POINT THAT PUZZLED. The Secretary (Mr. Charles Dukes) said he was inclined to think that the Council "ought to write back and say something th the Assur- ance Agents." The point that puzzled him was: What are the Agents going to do if they sever their connection with the Council? They could not act industrially; they could not come out on strike! No doubt their action was the result of the work of someone who was not in sympathy with the political outlook of the Council. This was not the first time the Coun- cil had been "let in" by that particular Union. Mr. Harrison moved that the decision of the branch be accepted.. _

Mr. Greely advised that the Council demand a summons meeting of the branch, and that a deputation be sent to wait on the Agents. He did not think that an 8 to 5 decision should be accepted as final, seeing the strength of the branch. They ought not to let the screw be put on by political parties; for that was what was being done. Instead, they should demand a summons meeting that should be thoroughly representative of the branch.

Mr. llamblett thought it might be as well to remind the Insurance Agents that they de- pended largely for their livelihood on trade unionists, and that if the members of the trade societies were to withdraw their support in large numbers the Agents would soon find them- selves in Queer-street. • Mr. Plinston (an honorary member of the N.U.L.A.A.) counselled the acceptance of the Agents' letter of withdrawal, stating that it was the principle of political action that was the root-bottom of the disaffection of the society in question. Upon a vote being taken, eleven voted in favour of opening up pourparlers with the Agents, and eighteen against', the proposition being, therefore, lost.

BELGIAN TEA.

Great Success of Novel Effort to Raise Money.

SACRIFICES MUST BE MADE.

Great success attended the Belgian Tea, which was held in the Parr Hall on Thursday. Stalls had been arranged in the body of the hall, and these were laden with numerous goods brought by the visitors and disposed of to others. The tea was served at the back of the hall, under- neath the gallery, and the attendants were busily engaged all the afternoon. During the proceedings, a band, got together by Mr. R. Whitfield, discoursed excellent music, and several artistes, including Miss Gladys Adams, Miss Longden, and Messrs. Gibbs (Run- corn), Poole and J. Babbitt, entertained the company. The accompanists were Miss Longden, Miss Newall, and Mrs. Gibbs (Runcorn). The tea was initiated by Mrs. Savage and Mrs. Hayward. who defrayed all the expenses, and handed the total proceeds to the Belgian Relief Fund. The ball had been decorated with Belgian colours, in addition to a large Union Jack, which was spread across the organ. Towards the close of the afternoon the Mayor (Alderman Peacock), together with the Mayor- ess, put in an appearance.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
PLEASED WITH THE RESPONSE.

Captain SAVA•GE, after expressing pleasure at the Mayor and Ilayoress's presence, said those who had organised the event were very pleased with the response. The arrangements had only been in hand for a week or ten days, and the committee were astounded to see so many pre- sent. They must not be stinting in these troublous days, and if they did not save any money this year, they must trust they would do so another year. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141017_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESSING NEED.

The MAYOR said the Belgian Relief Fund was one of the most pressing before the country. They had only to imagine Warrington being swept away by a foe who were nothing less than murderers, to know what it must be to the Belgians, in whose country all the towns and cities had been pillaged and destroyed. In the name of Warrington he thanked Mrs. Savage and Mrs. Hayward, Captain Savage and Mr. Hayward, the band, and the other ladies and gentlemen, who had helped the venture. The successful result showed that the right thing had been struck at the right moment.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Lost!

One of the refugees now in Warrington brought with her one of her children. A younger child, 31 years old, was staying with the husband's mother in Liege during the bom- bardment, and neither of them had been seen since. She did not know whether they were under the ruins of the house, or whether they were amongst the mass of humanity which was rambling about Belgium and France. That case was only one amongst thousands. They in England had a lot to be thankful for, and therefore they must not forget to do their part in relieving distress.

Increased Hospitality.

He had received a telegram from the Local Government Board, asking Warrington to in- crease its hospitality. A meeting had been held that afternoon, and several suggestions had been made, but it was only by events like that tea that afternoon that they could raise the necessary funds. If they were to increase their hospitality, Warringtonians would have to make sacrifices.

--.—„— He proposed a vote of thanks to all the work- ers, and this was heartily carried.

BRAVE BROTHERS AND SISTERS Mr. HAYWARD responded. Mrs. Savage and Mrs. Hayward thought they would do something for their brave brothers and sisters in Bel- gium, and as actions spoke louder than words, they decided upon organising the tea. (Ap- plause.) He wo glad to see the success which had attended the effort.

THE PROCEEDS. The proceeds amounted to about XllO.

JOTTINGS BY AK OLD JOURALIST.

Tha Sawbeth. The preacher at one of the harvest festival services at Holy Trinity bemoaned the fact that tha Sunday of to-day is not the Sunday of the people of the day when Waterloo was won. Just se. -Unless I have grievously- misread history - it is nowadays much more rationally and even 'reverently observed than was the case a hun- dred years ago. Anyhow, the Rev. W. Pollock- Hill cannot complain of Parliament desecrating tha Sabbath day--collectively, at all events. It is recorded that there have been only tiiteen Sunday sittings in six hundred years, exclusive, of course, of sittings commenced on a Saturday and continued into Sunday. Moreover, our Town Council meetings are never held on Sundays.

• "Opened by Censor." The experience referred to under this heading . by a special correspondent last Saturdv is now common. My daughter has for some time inter- changed letters in Esperanto with people in Russia, Japan, and Holland. Her Dutch friend is a teacher in Rotterdam, and the two fre- quently write to each other at length. Last weges packet had evidently been tampered with, and bore the explanatory words "Opened by Censor," and the communication showed that her previous one to Holland had been similarly treated. If the censor read it, as presumably I ha did, he would find abundant evidence of the sender's loyalty, for it happened for that once to consist in the flinging of several varieties of "beans" against the Germans, the intention being to discover on which side the sympathies of the Dutch girl lay.

Modern Antiques. In his lecture, " llow to understand pictures," Mr. Clarence Mawson incidentally observed that fabulous sums are paid for worm-eaten furni- ture. In a Lancashire town in which I lived eome years a well-to-do bachelor, of extremely mean and stingy disposition generally, devel- oped a weakness for antique furniture and spent considerable sums thereupon. When he died, the whole lot came under the hammer, and the experts attracted by the sale advertisements found that many of the things were spurious. "Worm-eaten furniture," quotha! A traveller who called at a cabinet maker's shop, and whose knocks on the counter elicited no response, was attracted to the back of the premises by the sound of pistol shots, and on venturing to in- vestigate he came upon the enterprising pro- prietor of the establishment industriously peppering a new piece of furniture with small shot to give it a worm-eaten appearance. Lots of so-called antiques are " faked " after that manner. •

Robbing Hospitals. The case of the boy charged with stealing a shirt from the Fever Hospital brought to my mind that of another misguided youngster whom I saw on one of my visits to the Eye Institution in Oxford-road, Manchester. He had been sent there from an industrial school, and one of the first things he did was to help himself to half- a-dozen penny postage stamps from a fellow- patient's locker and try to sell them to another inmate for 4d. After a while, he made up a bundle of clothes and escaped with this from the back of the hospital. He was challenged by an official, but stated that one of the nurses had given him the things to carry across to the laundry, and with this plausible excuse the ingenious youth got clear away. A hue and cry was raised, and, a policeman haying identified him from the published description, he was back at the hospital within a couple of days. When I saw him he was in bed, the sister in charge of the ward having deprived him of all but his nightshirt.

The Great Unpaid: The creation of new justices for Cheshire set me thinking. I have known quite a number who valued the distinction simply and solely because of the addition of the "J.P." to their /tames. I remember one of these, a cotton spinner, labelling his luggage with it, and then standing by it bn the railway station platform to note the anticipated impression on passers- by. In one flagrant instance which I recollect, the district had suffered through death or trans- ference of magistrates, and those of us who had felt the inconvenience particularly at Court sit- tings rejoiced when we learned that the Lord Chancellor had added half-a-dozen of the local gentry to the Commission of the Peace. How- ever, of those who had so long coveted the honour and at last achieved it, one immediately removed to the West of England and two others retired to the seaside. Nowadays, we have our ex-officio "beaks" to fall back upon—those entitled so to act as chairmen of urban and rural district councils—and it is as well.

Aeronautics. Warrington's recent glimpse of an aeroplane brought to my mind a lecture by an authority who noted many marked resemblances between present-day aeroplanes and the earliest flying animals, and concluded that an aeroplane could scarcely be described as a novelty because it had a certain similarity to some of Nature's failures that became extinct many millions of ' years ago. As someone has remarked, the art ftying and gliding is 80 deeply buried in historical rec'ord that we may assume its ear- lier developments arose from the texts of ancient myths. How thedalus and Icarus crossed the Egean Sea; Icarus, soaring higher than his parent, got scorched by the sun and fell, is an occurrence which pictures 'largely in the haze surrounding the dawn of Greek ancestry., The Prose Edda of the ancient Scandinavians have a glowing inspiration of flight. Grea, a goddess in the mystical world of Valhalla, was the messenger whom Frigga sent into the world on her errand of love. She was gifted with the Marvellous power of gliding over seas or through the air on her chariot, drawn by Hofrarpuir, the immortal horse. There is a regulation in the ancient law of Norway called "Gulathings Lagen "—"Let the Kim" and the • Bishops, with all possible care make inquiry 'after those who exercise pagan superstition; who make use of magic arts; who adore the genii of particular places, or tombs, or rivers. and who by a diabolic manner of travelling, are ttansported from place to place through the air." &c. There are many similar prohibitions—extend- ing over centuries—of "this diabolic manner of travelling." Marco Polo told astonishing stories of the flying machines of the Chinese, and references to flights from towers of men wearing wings are plentiful in mediteval romances. Nevertheless, until quite a few years ago, most of us were disposed to put the day of the most ambitious and determined aitlnan'Bl • success somewhere near the Greek Kalends. • When, after a long and tedious wait for suit- able weather for the experiment, Hubert Latham essayed the Channel passage, and dropped with his contraption into the sea; we laughed and said to each other "I told you, didn't _I?" And, when Louis Bleriot tumbled across on his dirty little monoplane, we said he had met with an extraordinary bit of luck that was unlikely to be repeated. Then came Selmet and Prier and a whole host of record- breakers; and now our Army and Navy flying men achieve wonders almost daily, and already familiarity has be,an to breed something like contempt.

Made in Germany. We still have to go to Germany for news bout our own country. The latest reliable report is to the effect that there is serious fric- tion between King George and Lord Kitchener, the former having become alarmed at the rais- ing of " Kitchener's Army." The War Minister, the King fears, is aiming at the throne, and it is now being recalled that Lord Kitchener was once told by a soothsayer "K. stands for Kin."—" Punch."
