IN THE COURTS.

STOLE A PURSE. Stiff Fine, for Pilfering Hay% her.

For stealing a purse containing 10s. in cash from the dwelling-house, 80, Wilderspool- causeway, Warrington, Frank Hughes, a young man described as a hawker, living at Brook Cottages, Lower Walton, was fined 1:2, with the alternative of a month's imprisonment, by the magistrates sitting at the Borough Police Court on Wednesday. From the evidence it appeared that Miss Annie Kilduff, to whom the purse and money belonged, had occasion to leave the house to go shopping. The purse and contents were left on the kitchen table. Prisoner, who was in charge of a greengrocer's cart, called at the back door and was alleged to have remained in the yard ten minutes. When Miss Kilduff returned the property forming the subject ot the charge was missing. Detective Summers told the Court how -that, accompanied by P.C. Skelton, he met the prisoner in Chester-road, and not being satis- fied with the answers he gave to their questions, he was taken to the Police Station, where he was charged. In reply to the charge he said "Not guilty !" Prisoner told the magistrates that he was only in the yard three--niinutes and did not take the money. The Mayor (who presided) said the Bench were satisfied that prisoner was guilty.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
• MISSING PRIZE MONEY. Thefts at Messrs. Crosfield's Works.

*At the Borough Police Court on Wednesday, Wm. Houghton (17), of 36, Piamere-street; Jack Lawton (15), of 200, Longford-street; and Arthur Goodwin (14), of 204, Longford-street, were charged with stealing, between August 26 and September 12, from Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons' works, a number of postal orders and .61 and 10s. notes. The Chief Constable said the boys had been employed on the clerical staff at the works. A number of postal orders and notes had been missed and observation was kept. One of the prisoners was taxed by Mr. Wardle, the secre- tary to the company, with regard to the thefts, but he denied all knowledge of them. As a matter of fact the shortage in money amounted to over .615, and the sum mentioned in the charge was redubed to .62 each in order that the boys could be dealt with by that Court. People, the Chief Constable explained, who sent in a number of soap wrappers to the firm were en- titled to some monetary consideration, and it was the money set aside for that purpose which had been stolen, for, instead of the the postal orders and notes being posted, they were pur- loined by the boys.

Several Complaints Receives. A number of complaints had beep received by the firm, and as a consequence cheques were sent out instead of notes, and during the time that practice was followed there were no com- plaints. Subsequently money notes were for- warded, and then more complaints were re- ceived. The police were informed of the thefts the previous day, and the firm decided to prose- cute. When the prisoners were formally charged, their answers were to the effect that Lawton and Houghton had taken the money and shared it with Goodwin.

Goodwin's father attributed his son's downfall to bad company, whilst Houghton's father said he did not know why his boy had committed the offence, as he had a good home. The Chief Constable said he ,was destred by Messrs. Crosfield's secretary to say that the directors of the firm considered the matter the previous day, and, whilst they had no desire to be vindictive, they thought they were bound to bring the matter before the magistrates. At the same time they had no desire to press the case, but he would like to point out that it was not a case of sudden temptation. Mr. Wardle told the Court that the firm had had numerous complaints with regard to the postal orders, the sums involved ranging from £1 to is. 6d. When the postal orders and notes were sent out it was the duty of the boy Houghton to place them into the envelopes, after which they were passed on to the boy who entered up the post. Then the envelopes were sent to the postal departmenefor dispatch.

A Witness's Suspicions. Lawton was engaged in this department, and he received a note from Houghton telling him which envelopes should be kept back. As the complaints were received from all over the country witness suspected that the thefts were committed in the office, and subsequently lie taxed one of the prisoners. The Chief Constable said that Detectice-Ser- geant Reddington, who had been making the inquiries, believed that Goodwin was not so deeply involved in the matter as were the other prisoners. The Mayor (who was in the chair) announced that Houghton would be fined ..Cl, with the alternative of a month's imprisonment. The Bench hoped that it would be a lesson to him, as they considered he was the ringleader. Law- ton was fined .12, with the alternative of a month in gaol, and Goodwin ..t1 or 14 days. The magistrates thought that the parents should have exercised more supervision over the boys.

Gets " Time " for Taking Time.—William Baxter, labourer, 17, Tanners-lane, was charged on remand with stealing on October 3rd a clock, valued at 25., the property of Margaret Mc- Cabe, from the lodging-house, 9, Stanley-street ; and Bridget Coney, alias Emily Baiter or Smith, of the same address, was charged with stealing a bed quilt Lind bed sheet, valued at Bs. 6d., from the same lodging-honse.—lt was stated that the parties had been living at the lodging- house in Stanley-street, and, when they left, the articles were missed. Prisoners had ad- mitted the offences. Some of the goods had been pawned.—Baxter said he was going to return the clock, which he took so that Ike would know the time.—Both prisoners were committed to gaol for seven days.

FRIDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Boi ton, Pierpoint and James Evans, Messrs. T. Locker, S. Wright, G. Pilling, W. Furness and A. J. Jolley. Theft of a • Bicycle.—James Astill, 18, Hale- street, was charged with stealing a gent's bicycle, value £l, the property of Eugene Geraghty, 58, Orford-lane.—The Chief Constable said that on Tuesday Mr. Geraghty left his machine outside the Union Inn, Winwick-road, and when he returned it was missing. Detec- tive-Sergeant Holland went to prisoner's house, after making inquiries, and saw the bicycle in the back kitchen. When interviewed, prisoner said, "I did not know what I was doing."—Pris- oner (to the Bench): I Was drunk at the time.— The Clerk: Why did you keep it afterwards?— Prisoner : I did not know whlcl7. way to get rid of it.—The Chief Constable: About 16 bicycles have been stolen in this way during the past three months.—Prisoner was bound over for six months, as the Mayor said, "to make a better man of yourself." "No Peace in the House."—John Henry Kelly, 27, Back Crossley-street, was summoned by his wife, Martha, of 3, Back Crossley-street, for being an habitual drunkard and she asked for a separation order.—Complainant stated that her husband was drunk three times every week, and there was no peace in the house with him.—For the defence, Mr. T. S. Steel said that defendant had a good character at Messrs. Guest Bros., where he had worked for 20 years. Mr. Guest had instructed him to appear for Kelly that morning. There was no proof as to defendant being an habitual drunkard:—The case was dis- missed.

FRENCH RELIEF FUND.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

- - - Sir,—Having been given to understand that much of the distress which exists amongst French people in London is being dealt with by other organisations, and having had their at- tention drawn to the greater amount of misery- prevalent in the French provinces which have been devastated by the German Army, the French Relief Fund have opened an office in Boulevard des Capucines, Paris, and are making arrangements to open a warehouse there for the reception of goods and provisions, so that henceforth we shall particularly direct our , , efforts to giving British assistance on the spot to the helpless and destitute women and children of our French Ally. We have been inundated with applications for relief from all parts of France, and from inquiries made by our representatives we may say that the distress and misery existing at the present moment amongst those unfortunate people who were forced to flee from their homes, and who are now without means of sub- sistence, is most pitiable and deserving of urgent assistance. To enable us to carry on our work, and to fight the ravages of sickness and the pangs of hunger, whilst the men are fighting the com- mon enemy, we are sadly in need of funds, and although we have already received considerable assistance, more money is still needed, and we request your kind co-operation by giving pub- licity to this appeal. Of all the claims which have been made upon British generosity, it is surely our duty to give some assistance to our brave Ally, France, whose men are now fighting our battles, and whose country has been over- run by our enemies. The committee will greatly appreciate con- tributions in cash, whilst clothing, provisions, blankets and other goods for distribution in France will also be thankfully received. ' All contributions should be sent to the Com- mittee, the French Relief Fund, 83, Pall Mall, S.W. It ought to be mentioned that so far as is known the French Relief Fund is the only organisation rendering British assistance in France. The Paris offices and staff have been gener- ously placed at the disposal of the committee, ree of all expense to the Fund. Pp. FRENCH RELIEF FUND, J. H. DICKINSON, Member of the Committee. 83, Pall Mall, London, S.W.

THE

ER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 17, 1914.

On the Bench.—The magistrates present at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday were: Messrs. R. C. Parr (in the chair), J. Hep- herd, C. Jackson Holmes, L. Greening, J. A. Ransome and W. Fletcher.

Parochial Committee.—Councillor Pilling pre- sided at the monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday even- ing and there were also present: Councillors Mclntyre, Bottomley, McDermid, Pearson and C. Hallows, with E. Bradshaw (clerk to Parish Council), W. H. Warburton (acting clerk) and S. Wright (surveyor).

Assault Case Dismissed.—At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Robert Bar- low, of Ellesmere Port, summoned Frederick Wilkinson, of Preston Brook, for assault, but the magistrates dismissed the case. The evi- dence of the complainant was that, arising out of a dispute, defendant struck him in the mouth and knocked three of his teeth out. This de- fendant denied.

C I erk-Sol d er.—At the Monthly meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday it was decided, on the proposition of Councillor Bot- tomley, seconded by Councillor Pearson, "to record our appreciation of the action of the Clerk (Mr. G. F. Ashton) in giving his services for his country. We wish him every success and hope he will be restored to us well and hearty."

Father a Wastrel: Mother in Gaol.—At the Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Jane Wright, a mar- ried woman belonging to Warrington, but of no settled place of abode, was charged with stealing a quantity of clothing, the property of Mrs. Eaton, Pike-street, Stockton Heath.— ' Superintendent Owen said prisoner had been employed by the prosecutris and that the articles were missed when she left the house. They were afterwards found to have been pawned.—The Police Court Missionary (Mr. . Laws) said he had repeatedly assisted the woman. Her husband was a wastrel, and the children of the couple were in the Workhouse. —Prisoner was sent to gaol for a month, with hard labour.

. Police Court Sequel to Motor Car Accident.— At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tues- day, James Hall, of Kingsley, Frodsham, was summoned for driving a motor car to the dan- ger of the public at Preston Brook on September 2nd.—Mr. T. S. Steel defended.—The evidence showed that as two Manchester youths were cycling towards Warrington from Frodsham, the defendant's car, which was alleged to be going at a high rate of speed, caught one of the cycles, knocking the rider off and carrying the machine a distance of 70 yards before it pulled up. It was further stated that defend- ant swerved to his wrong side, and as the cyclist darted across the road, in search of a safer passage, the defendant again swerved to his correct side, catching the cycle in the at- tempt.—Mr. Steel, for the defence, pointed out that a police witness had said that he had measured the distance from the side of the kerb to the tracks of the car-wheels and found a dis- tance of 9 feet clear, which left quite sufficient room for a cyclist to pass. The car was only going at 17 or 18 miles an hour, and the defend- ant, who was driving, was so upset at the accident that he got flurried, which accounted for his failing to pull up until he had gone some 70 yards beyond the scene of the accident. A mistake, the advocate submitted, was made by the youth when he thought that there was not room to pass.—The case was dismissed.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
THAT MOTOR CAR FIRE! Another Discussion by Parochial

Committee.

WAS THERE MUCH ADO ABOUT • NOTHING?

The Fire Brigade was again the chief subject of discussion at the monthly meeting of the Parochial Committee, held in the Council School on Tuesday evening. Councillor Pilling presided, and after Councillor Hallows had read the report of the Fire Appliances Committee, Councillor Pearson said that he had been informed by an expert that the Fire Brigade was efficient, but his informant had suggested that they should get some new hose. Councillor McDermid : We are better off than any other district round about. We are well equipped and able to attend to any small fire in our district. We have also a connection with the Wilderspool. Brigade, which makes us more secure. Councillor Bottomley : Has an explanation been given as to why there was no member of the Fire Brigade present at the motor car fire the other night?

Was There Much Ado About Nothing? Councillor Hallows: There has been much ado about nothing. I heard the bell ring and went to the door straight away, but before I got to the Square it had stopped. lam not a slow walker and I say the bell could not have been ringing more than a quarter of a minute. When I arrived at the Square the apparatus had been taken out. I followed the men, and between us we got a connection with the main. The motor ear which was on fire was out in the open and the hose-pipe reached right up to it, so that there was no need for the nozzle, about which there has been so much talk. Councillor Bottomley : I must reply to that. I say those statements are incorrect. One statement Councillor Hallows makes is that the motor car was away from the building. I ask Councillor Mclntyre, who was present at the Parish Meeting, to disagree with that state- ment. Is it a fact, Councillor Mclntyre, that it was away from the building? Councillor Hallows: I do not say that it was away at the start, but it was yards away by the time we got the hose to work. Councillor Bottomley : Now you make a qualifying statement. The Chairman: It is a statement as to bow he found the car when he got there.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
How Long Did the Bell Ring!

- Councillor Bottomley : The taxi-cab was on fire in the backyard of the house. The next statement to which I take exception, because it is untrue, is that the bell only rang for a quarter of a minute. I can produce witnesses in whom you can place the greatest reliance to prove to you that the bell was ringing for three or four minutes. Not only did it ring so long, but one gentleman present said: "Look here, are those young fellows having a game on, ring- ing the bell like this? The Fire Brigade has already gone up there." It rang three or four minutes, without question! Councillor Hallows: It is the firs‘time I have been called a liar. (Laughter.)

A Member: You are lucky ! Councillor Hallows: I came straight out of my own house. Where Councillor Bottomley came from I cannot say. The bell started ringing as I left my door. I did not go on my hands and knees to the Square, and when I got there the young men were half-way up to Lon- don Bridge with the handcart. Councillor Bottomley : That is wrong! I want this to be quite clear.

The Chairman: Next- business! Councillor Bottornley : I want to maxe it clear that the young men did not give up ring- ing the bell and run with the apparatus. Some of them did; but, even when the fire apparatus was at the fire, the bell was ringing still.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
" Likes to Look at the Funny Side."

Councillor Mclntyre: That is an exaggera- tion. _ _

Councillor Bottomley : Councillor Mclntyre thinks this is a playing matter, and lie comes here to laugh, jeer and make fun of matters. I will let you know that when I came from the car the bell was still ringing. Councillor Mclntyre has come across the man who asked if the young men were hav- ing a joke. Councillor Mclntyre likes to look at the funny side of a thing! Councillor Mclntyre: I say that, without doubt, Councillor Bottomley is exaggerating what took tilace as to the bell ringing. Why he does it, I do not know!

===== 0002601_19141017_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
SENTENCED TO DEATH.

Dog to be Destroyed for Attacking

Postmen.

The story of a dog with a peculiar antipathy to postmen was related at the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday morning, when Jane Grech, Dalby Villas, Acker's-lane, Latchford Without, was summoned for being the owner of a dangerous dog which was not kept under proper control. Superintendent Owen said that numerous complaints had been made by the postal author- ities with regard to the dog and on two occa- sions defendant had been cautioned. John Edward Shimmins, a postman, who re- sides at 98, Slater-street, Latchford, said that on June 28th he was delivering letters at the defendant's house, when the dog flew at him. Witness had a box in his hand at the time and with this he beat off the animal. The postal authorities had been compelled to stop the delivery of letters at the house for a time on account of the dog.

Bitten on the Thigh._

William Arthur Worrall, another postman, deposed that on June 12th he was delivering letters at the house when the dog went for him and bit him on the thigh. He used the letter bag to try to drive the dog away, but the animal grabbed it. Eventually the dog had to be taken away by one of the servants. Ernest Wrench, an inspector at the Warring- ton Post Office, said the delivery .of letters at the house was stopped until such times as a letter box was fixed on the outer gate. This was owing to complaints received concerning the dog. Superintendent Owen incidentally stated that a number of the postmen wilo had been calling at the house had been called away for service with the Army. P.C. Booth said that complaints about the dog had been made and defendant had been cautioned.

The Clerk (Mr. H. Greenell) : Why does the dog always attack postmen?—Witness : I do not know. (Laughter.) The Bench ordered that the dog be destroyed and that the defendant, who did not appear, should pay the costs of the case.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHER WHITLEY MAN FINED FOR "REFUSING TO QUIT."

At/ the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Fred Brocklehurst, of Lake Farm, Higher 14, bitley, was charged with being drunk on the licensed premises of the Birch and Bottle Inn at Whitley and refusing to quit the same when requested to do so. Mr. T. S. Steel, prosecuting on behalf of the Licensed Victuallers' Associativa, said that at about 6.30 on September 24th ° three men drove up to the public-house in a trap. A tervant seeing the condition of the defendant informed the landlord, who said it was no use the party pulling up there, as they would not be served. The defendant tumbled when trying to get off his seat. The party saw that they would not be served, so they drove on some little distance, when the defendant got out of the trap and entered the house without being observed. He said he wanted a drink and that he had been in the licensed business and understood what the law was. There were some other men in the room, and defendant, pointing to each individually, said: "Give that man a drink." The landlord refused to 'serve him, whereupon defendant said that he would help himself and went behind the bar, but fortunately did not toinli the taps.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Went When the Sergeant Came.

The licensee asked the man over and over again to leave, and eventually it became neces- sary to send for the police. Acting-Sergeant Bostock arrived at about eight o'clock, and the landlord, in his presence, again asked defend- ant to quit the premises, and that time he complied. However, before he went out he said to the Sergeant: "I have had something to drink and paid for it." He said that-with the idea of getting the licensee into difficulties, but, fortunately, there were men in the house who had seen all that had taken place, and they at once told the sergeant that defendant had had no drink in the place. James Cartwright (the liceniee) and Acting- Sergeant Bostock gave evidence bearing out Mr. Steel's statement.

Defendant, in his defence, said he was not drunk. He might have lost his temper 'a bit owing to the manner in which he was asked to leave. The licensee told him that if he did not go away he would " have him up at Dares- bury Sports." He denied that he went behind the bar; indeed, he did not believe there was room for two behind it. Defendant was fined Cl. and costs, Mr. R. C. Parr (the chairman) remarking that licensees must be protected.

SMAS,MED A GLASS PANEL.

Police Court Ending to Public-house Episode.

At the StockAon Heath Petty Sessions on Tue's- day, John Strir,ll, a farm labourer, of Antrobus, was charged with being disorderly on the licensed premisos of the Birch and Bottle at Whitley on September 20th, and also with re- fusing to quit, and, further, with doing wilful damage by breaking the glass door of the hotel. Mr. T. S. Steel prosecuted. He said that Mr. Cartwright, the licensee, was determined tp conduct his house i a proper manner and that any offenders should be brought before the magistrates. The defendant was not a cus- tomer at the house. On the day in question he came into the bar and entered into a conversa- tion with another man, becoming so heated that he used very bad language. The landlady refused to serve him because of his conduct, and he picked up another man's glass and threw it on the floor. The landlord then asked him to go, but he refused, and was only got out after a struggle. Wheil outside he rushed at the door and put his foot through one of the glass panels. Defendant denied that he wilfully broke the window. His hand, he said, went through it when he was being thrown outside. A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed, and de- fendant was , ordered to pay the cost of the damage.

S INGT

PADGATE.

Parish Church.—The harvest thanksgiving services were held' on Sunday, the preacher for the morning being the Rev. W. Bracecamp, of St. Paul's, Warrington, the evening service being conducted by the Vicar. In the afternoon a flower service was held, and the children were asked to bring pound offerings, which were afterwards sold, the proceeds being given to the waifs and strays. At the morning and evening services the choir rendered special harvest hymns and anthems, the soloists being Mr. Oddy and Master J. Young. There were crowded congregations at all the services. Mr. F. Blackwell presided at the organ. Wesley •Guild.—There was a good gathering at Monday's meeting, when a lantern lecture on " The Lil Islan'" was given by Mr. C. Wilcock- son. The lecturer gave a description of a cycle tour from Warrington via Liverpool to the Isle of Man, returning via Belfast, Fleetwood and Blackpool. The lecture was of special interest, , owing to the fact that all the views shown were produced from actual photographs taken by the lecturer. Takirfg Peel as a centre, he described the many beauties of the island, and well deserved the hearty vote of thanks pro- posed at the close of the meeting. The Rev. A. J. Morton presided.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM.

Congregationalists' Harvest Festival.—On Sunday the harvest thanksgiving services were held in the Lymm Congregational Church. The preacher for the day was the Rev. C. E. Watson, a former pastor, who is well-known and much respected in the district. The choir rendered special anthems at the morning and evening services in a creditable manner. In the after- noon a scholars' service was held, at which Mr. Watson gave an interesting address. Mr. Leather presided at the organ. The collections were very satisfactory. On Monday the fruit and vegetables were sold in the Schoolroom, the proceeds being devoted to the Church Poor Fund.

The Mysterious Sound.—Some considerable consternation was experienced in Lymm on Tuesday evening by the sound of a loud explo- sion, and an unusual sight in the heavens. Rumours of many kinds, and of varied natures, were afloat on Wednesday, and, as might be. expected, references to Germans were numerous. We know now that it was a meteor, and it is to be hoped that the nervous villagers have had their fears allayed.

Taken 111 in the Trenches.—Corporal Lewis, of the let Battalion Cheshire Regiment, and a former employe of Mr. F. Evans, the Cross, Lymm, has written a letter to his old master, in which he says that he went through several engagements with his regiment without receiv- ing a scratch. During the last battle he was in he was taken ill and had to be carried out of the trenches in an unconscious condition. When he came to he found himself, to his great sur- prise, in the Ist Southern Hospital in Birming- ham. A communication from a doctor dttached to the hospital states that the corporal is not wounded, but is ill.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
NO CONTEST IN LYMM WARD.

There is to be no contest for the vacant seat I in the representation of Lymm Ward on the Urban District Council. Mr. Alexander Mclntyre, of Burford-lane, and Mr. Bidder were both nominated, but the latter has decided to withdraw. Mr. Mclntyre, who was a member of the Council some years ago, has lived in Lymm for a good many years, and is well known and respected. His candidature was not backed by Only one political section, as the following names of supporters, taken from his nomination papers, will show : Messrs. J. T. Clarke, James Gould, K. Kershaw, H. Munro, W. J. Dunkerley, J. P. Lloyd, R. H. Gotobed, Geo. Bennett, C. Mars- land, F. Walton, C. Gleave and E. Landless.

HIGH LEGH.

Choir Outing.—The members of the High Legh Church choir and a number of friends joined in a pleasant outing to Blackpool on Saturday. Naturally, at this time of the year they found the popular_seaside resort less busy and attractive than usual, but, nevertheless, they managed to spend a most enjoyable day. Dinn6 and tea were-partaken of at a cafe near the North Shore, and at both meals the com- pany were joined by the Rev. T. H. and Mrs. Edwards.

MOORE.

Harvest Thanksgiving.—The harvest thanks- giving services were held on Sunday in the Moore Wesleyan Church. Mr. E. W. Evans, of Padgate, preached in the morning, and the Rev. 11. T. Dixon, of Penketh, in the evening. The choir gave two anthems—mo,rning, "The fruit of Thy works"; evening, "Ye are My flock." Miss Alice Lawton and Miss Mary Eustance took the solo parts. The church had been beautifully decorated with corn, flowers, and fruit, by the ladies of the congregation. Good collections were taken at each service.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
HELP FOR COAST TOWNS.

Mr. Stanley Wilson Expresses the

Gratitude of the Men.

The following letter has been received : Tynemouth Relief Committee, Town Hall, North Shields, 27th September, 1914. Dear Mrs. Edelsten,—l regret the delay in acknowledging the receipt of the parcel of clothing so kindly sent by the ladies of Warring- ton for the troops in this district. The name of the donor was not on the package, so I thought I would wait for an advice note to arrive, with the result that in the rush of work the acknowledgment has unfortunately been overlooked.

The gift was reported to my committee on Thursday last, when I was instructed to convey to the ladies of Warrington, through you ,an expression of our grateful thanks. We greatly appreciate your kindness, and I can assure you that the gift will be fully appreciated by the men.

As you are no doubt aware, we have an enor- mous number of troops in Tynemouth and the immediate vicinity, guarding the coast, and in addition we have a considerable number of recruits for training. The latter, almost without exception, are very badly equipped. We found it impossible to provide locally the number of shirts, socks and blankets which were required. On several occasions we have sent all our available stores to the Castle for the men in the forts, as the Military Authorities could not get their requirements from headquarters. The troops are drawn from all parts, and I am told they find the East Coast rather chilly, especially when on night duty. We expect about 4,000 additional troops on Monday or Tuesday. Arrangements have been made to billet these men in the schools and large hotels, so they will be provided for and made comfortable. I mention the above facts to show how grate- ful we are for gifts from inland towns, and I was personally delighted that my native town was the first to come to our assistance.—Again thanking you, I am, yours faithfully. STANLIT WILSON, hon. secretary. Mrs. Edelsten, President Ladle& Relief Com- mittee, Warrington.

WILL HISTORY REPEAT ITSELF?

By a coincidence, Warrington were last year, as they are this, drawn against Barrow in the first round of the Lancashire Cup Competition. When they met on October 18th, 1913, Warring- ton had the worst of the encounter, being de- feated by 15 points to 8.

ANGLING.

Warrington Working Men's

Association.

N.A. CENTRE.

Norton, Mickle Trafford, Chester (canal and river), and Beeston were visited by a fair mus- ter of members. No great catches were recorded from the Gowy or Chester, but further along Tattenhall-road way good catches werik caught, both Saturday and Sunday, by H. Moore; be- tween 71b. and 81b. was taken each day. The river was tried near Chestei, and the result was a blank. Several Manchester anglers, who had spent the week up stream, report that sport was entirely off, and the best of them had not a dozen decent fish for his week's angling. We have now to hand the results of the Northern Anglers' Grand final match, and it is the worst championship match experienced for years. The winner was found in R. Clarke, of Platt Bridge, the angler who won our local War Fund match.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

ASSOCIATION.

PENKETH ALBION v. BEAMONT UNITED. Penketh Albion entertained Beamont United at home on Saturday and sustained their first defeat. Winning the loss, Beamont played to- wards the Widnes-road end and soon became aggressive. Their left wing was a constant. source of trouble, and at last Beswick gave Mullery an opportunity, which he quickly accepted, a good shot opening the* score for Beamont. Soon after, Mullery transferred the ball to Critchley, who was well placed, and he added Beamont's second point. Half-time : Beamont 'United, 2 goals; Penketh Albion, nil. Penketh started the second half very well, but owing to a strong defence it was only after 20 minutes' continual bombardment that they scored. Their second goal, however, quickly came, and then, with the score two goals each, Beamont began to gain the upper hand. A smart clearance by Simcock saw Critchley break away on the right wing. He centred, and Mul- lery scored the winning goal for Beamont, who won 3-2. For the victors, the two full-backs (Dewar and Simcock) played a great part in Penketh's de- feat, as also did Critchley, Witton, Beswick, and Mullery. The losers were best served at , half-back.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
PADDINGTON UNITED V. RISLEY.

. Played on the United ground, before a good company of 'spectators. J. Brown (Fairfield United) and J. Chamberlain (Howley Athletic) made their first appearance for the home team. Risley commenced the game, and were early aggressive, the United goal narrowly escaping. Good combination by the home team transferred play to the other end, and Brown led a severe bombardment of the visitors' goal. The cus- todian frustrated all. attempts to score. Play continued to be fast and keenly contested by both sides. Pritchard and Holcroft were con- spicuous on the right, and the latter finished with a fine shot, which was well saved, while similar attempts from Brown, and Holmes missed by inches. The visiting pivot worked hard for an opening, but he was floored by Taylor, and left the field injured. Near the interval "another raid by the home team was successful, Barlow converting a good centre from Holcroft. Half-time: Paddington Res., 1 goal; Risley, nil. The homesters forced the pace on resuming, but they lacked finish, numerous openings going astray. The injured centre had now returned, but all his attempts to equalise were ineffectual against Chamberlain, who was very sound in defence. Risky were favoured with several free kicks, but they were not utilised. Pritchard gaining possession broke away and left the goal- keeper helpless with a fast low shot. The visitors were disheartened by this reverse, and were outplayed, only their goalkeeper saving them from a heavy defeat. ,Barlow added a third point in the closing stages, after tricking several opponents. A good game, contested throughout in/ the proper spirit, ended: Pad- dington. United Res., 3 goals; Risley, nil.

IRELAND, PRESENT AND FUTURE Mr. Redmsond's Appeal for United Action.

Mr. Redmond, addressing a meeting of Irish Volunteers and others at Wexford, said that so far as it rested with him he would not be content until every member of the volunteers was efficiently armed. lie warned them that without proper control an armed force was a danger to. itself and to its country. What they had got to do was to satisfy them- selves that the control of the volunteer organisa- tion was in proper, authoritative hands. The proper authority in every free country was surely the elected and recognised Government of the country. Mr. Redmond proceeded:— I have in my pocket a letter from Mr. Asquith respecting the pledge that an Irish Brigade was about to be formed, composed solely and offi- cered solely by Irishmen, and every man who individually felt it his duty to go to the front would go as a member of an Irish army. For my part I trust and believe that the manhood of Ireland will not be content only to remain at home waiting and watching while other men are risking their 'lives to defend their liberties, but in addition to defending the shores of Ire- land, will insist on maintaining bright and un- tarnished on battlefields of Europe the reputa- tion of our soldiery. Speaking subsequently to a general meetilig of people from different parts of the country, Mr. Redmond said that in his deliberate judgment the short delay in bringing the Home Rule Act into actual operation—delay caused by the war —would prove a blessing in disguise. Let them not be disturbed about the Amending Bill. "Our hands," said Mr. Redmond, "are com- pletely free, and I say that it is our chief busi- ness from this on to concentrate all our energies on making sure that the Amending Bill will be a real amending Bill and not a Bill for the mutilation or division of Ireland. I am not in the slightest degree disturbed by the threats of Mr. Bonar Law and Sir Edward Carson. Civil war between Irishmen was always, in my opinion, an impossibility. Its impossibility has now become patent to the whole world. The talk indulged in by Sir Edward Carson was childish and wicked. At this moment the high- est of all interests is to unite all Irishmen in the face of a great national peril, and it was , nothing short of criminal for so-called statesmen to come over uninvited to Ireland and deliber- ately endeavour to inflame feelings of sectarian- ism and sectional animosities which happily are fading away before a common duty and a common danger." Mr. Redmond concluded by inviting all Irish- men—north and south, Catholic and Protestant— to come together and devote themselves to the task of finding a way out on the Home Rule question.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
Fairfield Sustain Their First Defeat,

ELAIWOOD'S FINE SECOND-HALF RECOVERY.

The games in the Warrington and District League last Saturday proved very interesting, some keen struggles being witnessed. The climatic conditions were very good. The meet- ing of Orford White Star and Fairfield United resulted in a splendidly contested game, both clubs having now got strong teams together. The Fairfield Club had to bend the knee to the Stars, this being ,their first defeat this season. Although the game was played on the White Star ground, they could not claim to gain any advantage from this, as, after having to give up 1 their ground behind the Barracks, they had to seek fresh pasture, and it was not until late in the week that they were successful in obtaining the ground formerly occupied by the Orford St. [ John's Club at Long-lane. At Newton a very keen match was witnessed between the Cons. and St. Barnabas'. The [ visitors arrived with only eight players, and had [ to complete their eleven by including some of [ their supporters, whilst Shaw, their regular gaolkeeper, played centre-forward. Hinds, a former secretary of the St. Barnabas' Club, went between the posts, and by his good work prac- tically saved his side from defeat. The home team's inside trio-ihad several good openings, but preferred to finesse instead of shooting. The backs on both sides gave the best exhibition seen in Warrington and District League foot- ball since that body's re-formation. The game between the Central and the Bap- tists proved to be rather uninterest,ing, and in the end the first-named proved easy winners.. The venue of the match between Longford United and Earlestown Mission was changed to the Horse and Jockey ground, where the Mis- sion lads, after a very bard game, failed to keep up their form of the previous week, and had to retire defeated by the odd goal in seven. The match at Lovely-lane between the Recs. and Paddington was sternly contested from start to finish, and at the call of time the score was one goal each, 'the result being a disap- pointment to the visitors, especially in view of the splendid work put in by their defensive lines. Bushill, in the home goal, did very well, and was ably stipported by his backs, whilst Kendrick and Davies showed up best in the front rank. For the visitors, George, Holmes •and Spencer played splendidly, while Craik and S. Smith were the best of the forward line. Both teams are to be congratulated on their display and conduct on the field. If all teams showed the same spirit there would be no necessity for such reports as were brought for- ward at the last League meeting. The game at Padgate-lane between Fairfield A.F.C. and Hollins Green was being evenly con- tested, and no score had been registered, when, after fifty-five minutes' play, Perrins, one of the visitors' forwards, had the misfortune to break his leg and the game was at once abandoned.

Notes by 11 Linesman.”

which hit the upright and rebounded into play. The home defence cleared their area and an- other brief visit was paid to the visitors' goal. Play was being contested in mid-field when half- time was called, the visitors leading by 1 goal to nil. The second half opened very keenly, and the ball travelled quickly from end to end. The home forwards became aggressive, and Beres- ford, on the extreme right, got some good centres across, but the inside players muddled the chances thus afforded by holding on to the ball instead of shooting straight for goal. The visitors half-back line were kept very busy, and at length they set their forwards going, and Houghton and Vernon indulged in a good run, which got them to the home lines, but Dagnall proved very safe. After a spell of mid-field play, the visitors returned to the attack, and Walker missed a very, clear opening, but shortly afterwards Vernon made amends by beating Dagnall with a low shot. The home goalkeeper could hardly be blamed for this, as his view was somewhat obstructed. Following this set-back the homesters institute'd a strong attack, and the ball was centred well by both extreme wing men. But the visitors' emergency goalkeeper proved their salvation. He was called upon to save many times, and more than once he only succeeded in doing so at the expanse of corner kicks. At length the home inside players showed good combination, and tricky play got the visit- ors' defence mixed, which enabled Rhubotham to put the ball between the uprights, well out of the goalkeeper's reach. The homesters tried hard to get on level terms, but the visitors' backs defended most stubbornly. Dodds and Gleave shot wide after working into favourable positions. The visitors made brief visits to the home end, and Houghton had hard lines with a good drive, which hit the post. Just before time the homesters were unfortunate in a similar way, the ball hitting the upright and going out of play. The result was: St. Barnabas', 2 goals; Newton Cons., 1 goal.

BANK QUAY RECS. v. PADDINGTON. These teams met on Saturday on the Lovely- lane ground, before a large crowd of spectators. The following players lined up (under the supervision of Referee W. Bennett) : Bank Quay Recs.: Bushill; Berry and J. Smith; Welsby, Werrall and Millington; Eccleston, Long, Deakin, Davies and Kendrick. Paddington: George; Holmes and Spencer; Garnett, Wrench and Taylor; Scarisbrick, Bleasedale, Darbyshire, S. Smith and Craik. The visitors won the toss, and Deakin started the game. The visitors' goal was at once at- tacked, but the ball was sent wide. From the goal kick Paddington attacked strongly, and the home defence was severely tested, a corner hav- ing to be conceded. For some time the visitors had much the best of the exchanges, but were erratic in their shooting. Bushill and his backs had a very hard time, but were successful in keeping their charge. Darbyshire once broke past the backs, but made a poor attempt when in a good position. The Recs. woke up and made determined efforts to score, but George-in the visitors' goal saved many good shots. When half-time arrived no score had been registered. On resuming, the Recs. led off with an attack but Holmes relieved. However, they came again, but once more failed to pierce the de- fence. Then the visitors had a turn at the home goal, attacking very hotly. Craik and Smith showed up well, and, following a good run on the extreme wing by the former, the latter put in a shot which Bushill failed to reach. This had the effect of making both teams play even more keenly, and Kendrick and Davies were prominent with a good piece of combination, George Holmes and Spencer would no yield. At the home end Smith was only inches wide with a grand effort. Offside spoilt another effort by the visitors, and brought relief to the home defenders, who were beginning to show signs of distress. The home team were awarded a free-kick just inside the visitors' half. J. Smith took the kick, the ball, just when it appeared to be going over the bar, suddenly swerving and passing underneath, completely deceiving the visitors' goalie. The closing stages were contested at a very fast pace, hut no Ifurther scoring took place. Result: Bank Quay Recs., 1 goal; Paddington, 1 goal.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE. ORFORD WHITE STAR v. FAIRFIELD

UNITED. These teams met on the ground of the first. named at Long-lane, Orford. The following players turned out under the control of Referee B. Howell:— White Star: Miller; T. Pilling and Gregory; Caldwell, Denny and Gifford; Maddock, Meachin Dunbobbin, Hankinson and Allen. United: Potter; Woodhouse and Millward; Ditchfield, Hunter and Payne; Cox, Cole, Beb- bington, Poulter and S. Woodhouse. The home team won the toss and the visitors were set to defend the Lane goal. Bebbington set the ball rolling, and the home defenders were quickly called upon, when Miller saved very well. After good play in mid-field, the visitors again swarmed down on the home goal, and Poulter scored, after ten minutes' play. Following this reverse, the homesters made a strong effort to get on level terms, and were awarded a penalty kick, which was entrusted to Meachin, who shot over the bar. This brought relief to the visitors' defence, and Hunter, who was playing a good game at centre- half, gave Poulter some neat passes, and the latter, after getting past the backs, shot straight for goal, where Miller saved, at the expense of a corner. This proved fruitless, but the visitors continued to be aggressive, Bebbington, Cole and Ditchfield being very prominent in the matter of attacking. At half-time the United were leading by one goal to nil. On the resumption, the home team had a slight breeze in their favour, and they soon settled down to their game. Millward and Woodhouse in turn cleared their lines with good kicking. Caldwell, from long range, tested Potter, but the latter saved, and then Denny spoilt a good movement by handling. From the free kick Bebbington got dang;,,rous, but was defeated by Caldwell before he could put his shot in. From this point the Star forwards im- proved, and, again assuming the aggressive, en- abled Dunbobbin to make the scores equal. Shortly afterwards Gifford gained the lead for the Stars from a penalty kick. The game was now contested very strenuously by both sets of players, and an attack, led by Meachin, was spoilt by Allen getting off-side. The free kick enabled the visitors to make an incursion into the home quarters, Hunter and Ditchfield specially shining in the attack. The latter sent out to Cox, whose shot, however, went over the bar. Play was again at the visitors' end, and Potter had to save shots from Caldwell and Gifford. Denny, who was playing a grand game at centre-half for the Stars, put Dunbobbin in possession, when the latter was well placed, and he scored the third goal. The United, mainly owing to the good combined work of Hunter, Payne and Poulter, gave trouble to the home defence, and'a penalty kick was given for hands. Ditchfield took the kick, and placed the ball well out of the reach of Miller. As time was drawing near the visitors made determined efforts to get level, but the home defence pre- vailed. Result: Orford White Star, 3 goals; Fairfield United, 2 goals.

FAIRFIELD V. HOLLINS GREEN. The above teams met on Saturday,, on the Padgate-lane ground. The visitors won the toss and defended the railway goal. From the kick-off the visitors gained ground in the home territory, but were sent back, owing to Atkin- son being offside. The home team then made a raid on the visitors' end, and forced a corner. Etchells, the visitors' centre-half, made a grand pass to Perrins, who failed to gather the ball, and, in making an effort to recover himself, had tIA, misfortune to break his leg. Essenhigh, the assistant secretary to the Warrington and Dis- trict League, visited the injured player at- the Infirmary," and was pleased to learn that he was progressing as satisfactorily as possible.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, OCTOBER 17, 1914.

is considered to be a precautionary measure in view of the uncertainty of the political situation in Constantinople. According to advices from Bulgaria, the Yawuz Sultan Selim (Goeben) and the Medelu (Breslau), in company with other units of the Ottoman Navy, have been seen proceeding north- wards. Reports from Constantinople are increasingly unfavourable to the maintenance of Turkish neutrality. The Porte is stated to have super- seded the International Sanitary Commission in pursuit of its policy of completely emancipat- ing the country from the incidence of interna. tion al obligations.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESS OF THE

WAR.

Summary of Authentic News From All Involved Areas.

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR "THE EXAMINER."

WEEK.

progress, and that battle had been joined along the line of the Vistula from Iwangorod to San- domir.

The Russians had re-established themselves at Skiermewiec, 40 miles to the south-west of War- saw.

According to the Berlin wireless circular the capturing of Russian prisoners goes on as mer- rily as ever, but it is remarkable that the Ger- man progress is far from worthy of so many victories. Even on the hurried retreat from the Neimen many thousand Russians were taken, an unusual achievement for an army so fast to the rear as the Germans then were. _ _

On the left bank of the Vistula, during the battle which took place on the 13th inst., the Russian troops repelled the German attacks directed on Warsaw and Ivangorod. Fighting is taking place to the south of Przemysl.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
ELEVENTI FALL OF ANTWERP

GERMANS ATTEMPT TO OVER- RUN BELGIUM.

Antwerp was evacuated on Friday last.week, after a -bombardment by heavy guns that com- pletely outranged the guns at the disposal of the defenders

Before the end came and afterwards terrible scenes were witnessed in the neighbourhood of the town. Roads were packed with weary men, women, and children, fleeing from the terror of bombardment and making pathetic efforts to carry with them such of their goods as they had been able to rescue from the wreck of their homes.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH ASSISTANCE.

When the end came it was announced that in response to an appeal by the Belgian Govern- ment, a Marine Brigade and two Naval Bri- gades, about 8,000 men, together with some heavy naval guns, manned by a detachment of the Royal Navy, the whole under the command of General Paris, R.M.A., were sent by His Majesty's Government to participate in the de- fence of Antwerp during the last week of the I attack.

They performed their duty most gallantly and resolutely. They offered to cover the retreat of the garrison, but General de Guise preferred that the last troops to leave the city should be Belgian. Two of the brigades have reached Ostend safely with all the :leavy guns, but the let Naval Brigade were cut off by the German attack west of Antwerp, and 2,000 officers and men entered Dutch territory and laid down their arms.

An Amsterdam report states that over 20000 Belgian troops were compelled to retreat into Dutch territory. The Germans are reported to have demanded a war contribution of .C 20,000,000. The German commander, General Baseler, has issued a proclamation to the remaining inhabit- ants warning them against "refractory con- duct," which "may lead to the demolition of your beautiful city."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
GOVERNMENT REMOVED TO FRANCE.

The Belgian Government has arrived at Le Havre, where its members were welcomed in the name of France by M. Augagneur, the French Minister of Marine.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STRUGGLE IN FRANCE.

GREAT GERMAN - EFFORT TO BREAK THROUGH. ALLIES STAND FIRM.

Almost simultaneously with the fall of Ant- werp, Lille, in the north of France, was occupied by a German Army Corps, and there were indi- cations that a supreme effort was to be made to sweep the coast in Belgium and break through the Allies' defence by a turning movement on the left. The exact position of the new front in Flanders was uncertain, but it appeared that the enemy was in force at Selzaete and at Gbent, while his cavalry was in action at Thourout, Dixmude, Ypres, Hazebrouck-, and Bethune. Better news came on Thursday, when it was reported that the Allied troops had occupied Ypres, in Belgium, which was recently in the possession of the Germans. In fighting which took place near Ghent on Monday and Tuesday, it was announced that British troops had taken part. Cavalry engagements continued in the region to the west of Lille and are believed to extend as far west as Bailleul.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
MARKED PROGRESS ON THE FRONT.

There has been a marked decrease of vitality on the part of the enemy on the front and on the lower part of their right wing. The proba- bilities are that men had been largely with- drawn for concentration in the neighbourhood of Lille.

The French gained "some real advantages " at several points north of the Oise. Slight progress was announced on the old en- trenched front north of the Aisne, "particularly north-west of Soissons," where the British forces on the Aisne are supposed to be. Attacks on the Allied lines in the neighbour- hood of Lassigny and Roye have had no success. Between Arras and Albert, the French made marked progress, as also between Laon and Reims.

At the eastern end of the line the German attack south-east of Verdun was renewed on Friday night and Saturday, with great violence, but the French, after losing and retaking the village of Apremont, were at the finish holding their ground.

- On the Upper Meuse the attempts of the Ger- mans to recover the ground which they had steadily been losing during the past ten days were, in spite of violent efforts on their part, fruitless.

A recrudescence of activity is officially re- ported in the Vosges, where a German offensive to the north of St. Die has been repulsed. In the Woevre it appears that the Germans are not in a position to attack the Verdun forts, in spite of the assertions of Berlin to the con- trary.

ALLIES GAINING GROUND. Germany advantage gained at Antwerp. Her troops are is pushing with feverish energy the marching westward and had reached iati Wednes- day evening the Bruges and Thielt district. Ostend was threatened and large numbers of refugees have arrived in English. ports' from Belgium.

News from France is good. On the Allied left, where some British troops are now engagrd, notable progress has been made about Lens, Arras and Albert. Further north, save that the enemy have left the Channel banks of the Lys, the Scheldt's most westerly tributary, the posi- tion is unchanged. Near Rheims in the centre and beyond the Meuse, where the Verdun-Metz road has been aproached, the French have ad- vanced. • PROGRESS MAINTAINED. Yesterday afternoon it was reported that the progress which was indicated by Thursday's communication is confirmed. On our left wing the action of the Allied Forces extends from the region of Ypres to the sea. _ _

===== 0002601_19141017_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA AND GERMANY.

SECRECY AND CONFLICTING REPORTS.

Some uncertainty exists as to the disposition of.the forces arranged in Poland, but this week the Germans appeared to occupy a front from near Mszconow, 26 miles from Warsaw, and to the south of the railway from that capital to Skierniewice, and Grojec nearer to the Vistula. In the south an Austro-German force has ad- vanced along the left bank of that river from a spot due north of Tarnow to a front between Opatow and Sandomir. The Russians were holding the line of the Vistula from Sandomir to Iwangorod. According to a " Times " Petrograd corre- spondent the hostile forces in Poland amount to 28 army corps. It appears that the Germans have no great confidence in • the fighting qualities of their Allies, as they are reported to have taken the precaution to brigade Austrians with German troops. Russia has been very quiet, but has claimed some successes, which on the other han'd Ger- many and Austria have denied. The Russian assertion that attache by the enemy had been repulsed suigested that the reinforced German Army had been trying, to take revenge for its defeat on the Niemen. News from French 'sources asserted that the German retreat into East Prussia was still in

,

===== 0002601_19141017_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INVASION OF HUNGARY.

Reports from Hungary seem to show that the Russian movement through the passes of the Carpathians was something more than a mere raid. Telegrams from Rome and Bukarest indi- cate that the Russian invasion of Transylvania is more formidable than the Hungarian Govern- ment at first affected to believe.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
PRZENISYL.

Reports of Relief Contradicted. The Austrians claimed to have repulsed a Russian attack on Przemysl, which was relieved. On the other hand, it is reported that an ad- vance on the part of an Austro-German force in Galicia in the direction of Przemysl has met with a sharp repulse. This engagement con- sorted ill with the Austrian announcement that the siege of Przemysl had been raised, a state- ment which is further discounted by the Rus- sian details of the progress of their attack upon that fortress.

The Petrograd official report says the position is unchanged in East Prussia, on the Middle Vis- tula, and on the San. In Galicia, south of Przemysl, a Russian column has defeated the Austrians and taken from them 300 prisoners and several machine guns.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH AIR RAID

SUCCESSFUL ATTACK ON ZEPPELIN

SHEDS.

The Naval airmen have carried out another successful raid on the Zeppelin sheds at Dussel- dorf—a task requiring the greatest enterprise and daring, since the attack made some time ago has no doubt put the defence of these vul- nerable places on their guard. The attack was made by Squadron-Commander G. A. Spencer-Grey and Lieutenants R. L. G. Marix and S. V. Sippe. Bombs were dropped by Lieutenant Marix from a height of 500 ft. They pierced the roof of the airship shed, and flames shot up some hundreds of feet into the air, evidently as the result of the gas of an air- ship being set on fire.

The airlen are safe, but their aeroplanes have been lost.

GERMAN BOMB THROWING. Encouraged by their success in wounding a little girl on Thursday. German aviators on Sunday dropped twenty bombs over Paris, kill- ing three persons and wounding 14. The roof of the Cathedral of Notre Dame was struck. _ _

Other bombs were dropped on Mon-day on Paris by a German airman. No great damage was done.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
REBELLION IN SOUTH AFRICA.

RESULT OF GERMAN INTRIGUE.

In South Africa Lieutenant-Colonel Maritz, who was in command of the British forces operating against German South-West Africa, has produced a German general's commission, and, after arresting the loyal officers and men of his command, has taken up his new duties and sent an ultimatum to the Union Govern- ment.

It is obvious that this open rebellion, says the "Times," is the result of very careful and con- sidered plotting on.the part of the Germans, and, as such, is an illuminating example of the laborious underhand preparations made by Ithe enemy during the years of peace for the discolu- fiture of Great Britain when the day should dawn. Apart from the moral satisfaction that Mr. Maritz's action will undoubtedly afford in Ber- rin, his defection can have but little effect upon the military situation in South Africa. It has, moreover, already produced gratifying demon- strations of loyalty throughout the Union, where Afrikander opinion on all sides is outspoken in condemnation of so shameful a betrayal. The Government has declared martial law. Later news shows the rebellion to be still more insignificant from a military point of view than was at first supposed. Steps are being taken throughout the Union to counteract any possible ramifications of the German conspiracy, and ecclesiastical influence is being brought to bear in the same direction.

INDIGNATION ROUSED. Recruiting in South Africa for the Army under the command of General Botha is proceeding briskly. The revolt of Maritz has had its in- evitable result in arousing the indignation of loyal South Africans, Dutch and English. The Legislative Council of Southern Rhodesia has requested the Government to raise a con- tingent of 1,000 men to be equipped and main- tained out of the proceeds of new taxation. General Botha will take the field much sooner than he had intended, and will place himself at the head of a strong force composed in part of Boer commandoes. Several disaffected persons, among them German agents, have been arrested under martial law. Many plots against the Empire are believed to have been hatched, but the situation is well in hand, and there is no ground for anxiety. The rush to enlist in the Union's regiments continues.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
AT SEA. RUSSIAN CRUISER SUNK. German Submarines' Attach in the

Baltic.

On October 10, in the Baltic, German submar- ines unsuccessfully attacked the Russian cruiser Admiral Makaroff. On the following day, how- ever, submarines again attacked the Russian cruisers and sank the Pallada with all hands.

EMDEN'S ASSISTANTS CAUGHT. German means of communication on the high seas are gradually being destroyed by the ac- tivities of British warships. The Admiralty an- nounced that ILM.S. Yarmouth, Captain Henry L. Cochrane, has sunk the German liner Markomannia off, Sumatra and has captured the Greek steamer Pontoporos. Both these ships have been engaged in coaling the Emden, and were with her during her raid on British commerce in the Bay of Bengal last month.

CAPTURE OF WIRELESS STATION. The Secretary of the. Admiralty states that a communication has been received by the Gov- ernment of the Commonwealth of Australia from the Administrator at Rabaul, reporting the cap- ture of the small German sailing vessel Comet, which was found to have a complete wireless telegraph station on board.

BRITISH CRUISER SUNK.

ONLY 52 RESCUED.

The Secretary of the Admiralty makes the following announcement :

- Thesus (Captain Hugh Edwards, R.N.) was attacked by a submarine in the Northern waters of the North Sea on Thursday afternoon, but was missed. H.M.S. Hawke (Captain Hugh E. Williams, RN.) was attacked about the same time and was sunk. , The following officers, together with 49 men of the crew, have been landed at Aberdeen from a trawler:—

Mr. Sidney Austin, boatswain; Mr. James Dennis, gunner; and Mr. Harry G. T. Evitt, acting gunner.

The remaining officers and men are missing. The Admiralty announces that Lieutenant- Commander Kosoman and 20 men of H.M.S. Hawke have been saved from a raft. The llawke was commissioned in 1893, and was the oldest of 17 protected, cruisers in the Navy list. She had a displacement of 7,350 tons and had attained a speed of 20.9 knots. She carried two 9.21 n., ten 6.in'. and 17 smaller quick-firing guns, together with two machine guns. The Hawke cost £400,702 when built.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
ROUIVIANIA.

DEATH OF THE KING.

The death of the King of Roumania, which took place suddenly on Saturday, may have an important bearing on the continuance of Rou- mania's neutrality in the war. A prince of the house of Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen, King Charles was bound by family and personal ties to the Royal House of Prussia, and as the move- ment rose in Roumania for joining in the war against Austria he was understood to have de- clared that he would rather abdicate than sign a declaration of war against Austria and Ger- many. The new King is a younger son of King Charles's elder brother, Prince Leopold of Hohenzollern-Sigtnaringen, is 49 years of age, and for 25 years has been the heir presumptive. King Ferdinand issued on Sunday a proclama- tion that he would follow in the steps of his pre- decessor and realise the ideals of the Roumanian race.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
ITALY.

A correspondent in Rome reports that it is now difficult for Italian-speaking Austrian sub- jects to leave Trieste. The " Messagero" under- stands that the Lord Lieutenant of the Littoral has selected hostages among the Italian-speak- ing population to be arrested in case of war with Italy.

COMFORTS FOR OUR SOLDIERS. GOODS POUR IN, BUT NEED STILL

GREAT.

Mrs. Edelsten reports : Thursday was an exceptionally good day at the Town Hall, from the large number of really good, well-made articles that poured in-382 in all. A splendid contribution (the third dona- tion) came from the Women's Liberal Associa- tion, per Mrs. Osborne 4 One ,hundred and thirty- two warm woollen and flannel garments went to Lady Jellicoe for the use of the Navy. A parcel of hospital requirements and magazines went to Fazakerley ; 70 articles went to Lady Barr's depot in Liverpool, for the use of hospitals at the front; 72 socks and belts for Warrington sol- diers went to the Conservative Club in exchange for a bundle of scarves.

Mrs. Pemberton gratefully accepted a large bundle of new and second-hand clothing for the Belgians at St. Austin's.

Tiae need is still great : warm clothing of all descriptions will be gladly received.

PAY FOR WIVES OF SOLDIERS. How to Rectify any Cause for Complaint.

There have been complaints respecting the subposed inadequacy of the amounts that have I been paid to the wives and families of soldiers. The War Office, who naturally have had a diffi- cult task to meet all the work that has been suddenly thrown on each department, are get- ting the question of allowance settled on a satis- factory basis. Much of the misunderstanding has arisen because of the uncertainty as to the allotments made by the men themselves as to allowances from their pay for -their wives. We are asked to say that any woman who does not get the right amount will have her allow- ance put in order if she will apply to the offi- cials at the Parr Hall. It is not necessary to go to the trouble of writing to the War Office. WIVES' PEACEFUL RAID ON THE POST

OFFICE. Quite a raid was made by the wives and de- pendents of our gallant defenders upon the War- rington Post Office on Monday, the first day for the paying out of the new separation allow- ances.

The messenger boys' room was turned- into I the distributing centre, the applicants being ad- Mated in batches. During the day over 800 cases called for the attention of the officials, and 1,000 per week will probably prove to be the future average. Dependents can receive their money on any clay during the ordinary hours that the Post Office is open for money transactions. More- over, if it is more convenient, the allowances may be obtained at a sub post office, by the filling up of a transfer form. OVER £l,OOO IN LOCAL RELIEF. Yesterday an "Examiner" representative was informed , that during the week, the Soldiers' and Saflors' Families' Association, whose local office is at the Parr Hall, had relieved about 1,000 Warrington families. This relief has now been in operation for ten weeks, during which time assistance has been afforded in over 1,250 local cases. The total sum paid amounts to over £l,lOO.

Meant Well. • An Irish recruit was once brought up for breaking into barracks—that is, getting over the wall instead of entering by the gate. "But, Murphy," said the Officer, ." though you were late you should have come in by the gate." " Plaise, yer honour," said Murphy, "I was afraid of waking the sentry."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

MERRY MUSICAL MELANGE AT THE ROYAL COURT.

"The Pride of Byzantia "—a vastly enter- taining hybrid between musical comedy and pantornime—is drawing big crowds to the Royal Court Theatre this week, and deservedly so, for a right merry trifle, full of fun and lilting Tags, which latter, needless to say, include in ir number one or two stirring patriotic items a species of lyric effort which makes an irre- sistible appeal to all hearers in these days. The piece goes with a swing from start to finish, and what it lacks in plot—and who wants a plot in this sort of production?—it makes up for in music and merriment. Since the play was last staged .at the Royal Court it has undergone revision, with the result that it is now, in the words of the showman, "slap-bang up-to-date !" The principal fun-maker is Mr. Will Lindsay, who, as "Sammy Blenkinsop fm' Yorkshire," is a host in himself, and keeps the risible facul- ties of the audience busy the whole time he is on the stage. Mr. Charles-Tolcher, as "Adolph from Paris," helps nobly in the task of laugh- ter-raising, and Messrs. James Chippendale and Albert Cavendish also do their best to add to the swelling chorus of chuckles. Miss Madge Soutter as "Sarah Ann," makes the most of the part ot a skittish old dame, and Miss Minnie Myrle is charming as "Mabel Rivers," her vocal contributions to the merry medley of song, dance and witticism including "Missis- sippi Lou," in which she has the assistance of Little Mary O'Hara (who plays the part of a Waif) and of the Eight Sunbeams, an octette of winsome wee lassies, who dance and sing in a manner that captivates all hearts. Miss Mabel Medrow acts cleverly, dances gracefully, and sings charmingly in the title-role, and it is interesting to know that she has Leon engaged to succeed no less a star in the theatrical firma- ment than Miss Clarice Mayne in the next pantomime at the Theatre Royal, Leeds. The remainder of a long caste is well filled and the piece is sumptuously staged and dressed, and it can be conscientiously recommended to all in search of a harmless two-hour frivol.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

As usual, the programme submitted at the Hippodrome this week is an excellent one, being pleasantly varied and highly entertaining. The "turn" provided by Captain Fred 'Woodward's sea lions and seals is strongly reminiscent of the circus ring of our boyhood, and it is proving decidedly popular. The seals are described as "the most wonderful performing animals," and seeing that they belong to one of the lowest fornis of creature life and have been trained to sing, juggle and play "football" with their snouts, one cannot but think that the descrip- tion has more of the virtue of truth about it than does many a showman's announcement. A pathetic story of humble life is embodied in the sketch "The Sneak Thief," which is ably presented by May Stafford Smith and company, Miss Smith being very highly successful in the title role. Dacre and Lynton sing remarkably well and their musical scene, "The Tenor and the Burglar," is very pleasing. Nerve is a clever hat manipulator, with a host of new tricks, and his eccentric acrobatic dancing is most skilful. Adkin is a smart ventriloquist, whose gags and stories are new and witty. Kitty Dale, a musical .comedy star, pays a first visit to the hall, and is very well received, her singing being highly effective. The Holsons are patter comedians and simultaneous dancers, with a style of their own, and they are loudly applauded for their efforts. Next Week's Attractions.

"The Courtiers," in a musical-comedy scena, top a good bill for next week, and other turns will be given by the Bradshaw Brothers, eccen- tric acrobats; Josephine Langley, lady ventrilo- quist; Victor Wakeman, "the human gramo- phone"; Melt and Bray, comedians; Damerell and Rutland, comedians; and Mr. and Mrs. Manning in "Our Wedding Anniversary."

===== 0002601_19141017_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
PICTUREDROME.

A programme replete with good things was submitted at the Picturedrome on the first three nights of this week. " The Barrier of Ignor- ance" was the "star" film, and it proved to he a powerful drama, in which the various drama- tic situations were exceedingly well portrayed. Laughter came without effort at the clever comedy "Outlined and Outwitted," which pic- tured the scheme by which a silhouette artist got "pa's consent" on behalf of a fellow crafts- man. " Sisters " was the title of a film tale of human interest, and which was much appre- ciated. The inimitable and bulky John Bunny, whose facial control, allied to his heroic dimen- sions, has proved his fortune, made his appear- ance via the admirable .comic film, "Bunny's Mistake.s A beautifully tinted and lifelike pic- ture, showing river scenes in Normandy, was much admired by all lovers of the beautiful in Nature, and was a fine illustration of the pitch of perfection to which the bioscope photographer has brought his art. The pictured story of a committee desirous of stamping out vice, and which to that end raided a gambling house, took the gaming machines, and who were "Caught with the goods," proved very humorous, and was responsible for more than one hearty peal of laughter from the audience. The ninth of the series of films depicting the adventures of "Lucille Love," was of a most interesting description, the fight on the roof, which was the outstanding incident, proving most exciting. The management of the theatre have thought- fully arranged that members of his Majesty's forces, when in uniform, shall be admitted at half-price. German Spy Film Next Week. The star-film, in the programme for the early part of next week will be "The Reformation of Calliope," which deals with the "making good" of a Western settlement scallywag. For the remainder of the week "0.11.111.5., ' which has been specially produced since the com- mencement of the war, will be the principal attraction. It tells a thrilling story of Ger- man espionage in England.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
PICTURE HOUSE.

Visitors to the Picture House invariably come away pleased with the programme submitted for their delectation, and that gone through at ,the beginning of the week was no exception to the rule. "Shot at Midnight," which headed the " bill," proved to be a drama containing many clever situations. It was full of interest and excitement, and could be classed amongst, the best of this kind of film story. The comic element was not overlooked in the arrangement of the programme for the first part of the week, the "Haunted Hotel," "Bad Game," and "The Vapour Bath" being the titles of really first- rate comedies. "The Spirit of Madonna" and " The Price of Vanity" were two dramas of thrilling interest well worth seeing, whilst "Frolicsome Bears" was an excellent educa- tional subject.

Local War Film Next Week. The big item in next week's programme will be the screening of a film showing the latest recruits for the 4th Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment and the members of the Warrington and District Preparatory Train- ing Corps undergoing their training. The pictures have been specially taken for the enterprising management at considerable trouble and expense. "The String of Pearls" is the title of a fine drama-film secured for the first three nights, and on the last three nights "The Finger Prints" and "The Counter- feiters" will form the big attractions.

===== 0002601_19141017_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA.

Two fine bills of fare were Placed before patrons at the Star Kinema this week. Many excellent draniota films were shown .during the first three days, included in their number being "A Leech of Industry" and "Stolen Plans," both of which dealt with the business world; Whilst "The Other Man" provided drama of a strong domestic interest. A splen- did issue of the "Gaumont Graphic" included a special, "In the Wake of the Huns," deal- ing with the war abroad. An entertaining comedy film, "Mack at it again," provided the humorous element • The second attractive programme, now show- ing, includes. "The Defence of Most"; the problem-film, "Was She Justified?"; and an- other instructive war picture, "Stricken Belgium." Next Week. A topical film depicting "The Fall of Ant- werp" will form the chief attraction at the Star Rinenaa daring the first half of next week, Whilst "The Sack of Termoncle" and "The Battle of Lebeke" figure in the programme for the remaining three days.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. EUROPEAN WAR. TOBACCO FUND FOR THE 2ND SOUTH LANCASHIRES (THE WARRINGTON LINE BATTALION) SERVING AT THE FRONT. The first consignnaent of tobacco, consisting of 70,000 cigarettes and 2,000 cakes of tobacco, at a total cost of £45, has been despatched to the Front. Further subscriptions are invited for a second consignment to be despatched during November. G'entlemert are requested to give their old and discarded pipes, which should be sent to the Town Clerk. COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. BELGIAN REFUGEES. The Local Committee have been requested to ' provide further accommodation for Belgian Refugees, and they invite offers of houses or , other buildings (preferably within the Borough)1 for the accommodation of the poor victims of the war. Further gifts or loans of beds, bedding, linen, furniture, clothing, &c., are urgently appealed for. Sixteen refugees have been accommodated at St. Austin's, and contributions of money, food and clothing are requested for their mainten- ance. Ladies and gentlemen willing to help may ascertain the best way of doing so by inquiring at St. Austin's on any day except Saturday and Sunday; between the hours of 11 and 12.30. Subscriptions to the " War Refugees Fund" should be sent to the Borough Treasurer at Bank House. PETER PEACOCK, llfayor. SUCCESS. What i the secret of Success? asked the Sphinx. "Push," said the Button. 'Take pains," said the Window. 'Never be led," said the Pencil. Be up-to-date," said the Calendar. Always keep cool," said the Ice. "Do business on tick," said the Clock. "Do a driving business," said the Hammer. "Aspire to greater things," said the Nutmeg. "Make light of everything," said the Fire. "Do work you are suited for," said the Flue. "Get a good pull with the ring," said the Doorbell. "Be sharp in all your dealings," said the Knife. "Find a good thing and stick to it," said the Glue. "Trust to your stars for success," said the Night. "Strive to make a good impression," said the Seal.

WEST LANCASHIRE DIVISION Transport and Supply Column. • SOUTH LANCASHIRE BRIGADE COMPANY ARMY SERVICE CORPS. Men are still Wanted to complete the Company being raised to replace the Company that has volunteered for Active Service. Saddlers, Farriers, Motor and Horse Owners and Drivers are particularly required. (Clerks, Warehousemen and Artisans of any trade will be accepted and trained as Drivers.) A Recruiting Officer will attend at Head- • quarters, 46, Legh Street, each evening between 7 and 9 o'clock, until the required men are enlisted.
